1:                                                                              Chiral vector bundles
2:                                                                                        Timothy Eller
3:                                                                                    November 14, 2018
4: 
5: 
6: 
7: 
8: arXiv:1004.3081v1 [math.QA] 19 Apr 2010
9:                                                                                            Abstract
10:                                               Given a smooth G-vector bundle E → M with a connection ∇, we propose the construction of a sheaf
11:                                           of vertex algebras E ch(E,∇) , which we call a chiral vector bundle. E ch(E,∇) contains as subsheaves the
12:                                           sheaf of superalgebras Ω⊗Γ(SE ⊗ΛE) and the sheaf of Lie algebras generated by certain endomorphisms
13:                                           of these superalgebras: ∇, the infinitesimal gauge transformations of E, and the contraction operators ιX
14:                                           on differential forms Ω. Another subsheaf of primary importance is the chiral vector bundle E ch(M ×C,d) ,
15:                                           which is closely related to the chiral de Rham sheaf of Malikov et alii.
16: 
17: 
18:                                           Contents

97: transfers the action of a vector field X on a smooth function f to the vertex algebra (specifically,
98: Xf = X ◦0 f ), it does not transfer the product of f with a vector field X to the vertex algebra (so
99: f X 6= f ◦−1 X in general). Our amendment is to impose the relation f X = f ◦−1 X. However, this
100: does not come without a trade-off. To wit, although we are able to prove in some specific cases that a
101: souped-up Lie algebra injects into the vertex algebra it generates, this injectivity is only conjectured to
102: hold for arbitrary souped-up Lie algebras, including for s(a ⊗ sX∇ , a).
103: 
104: 1.1.2    Relation to other vertex algebra bundles
105: Ours is not the first discussion of vertex algebra bundles. There are two others of which the author is
106: aware, and both of those are substantially different from the one at hand. The one more closely related
107: is that of [DLMZ04], who have defined a notion of a K-theory associated to vertex algebras. Their idea
108: is to begin with the concept of a V -bundle on M , which is akin to a G-bundle but with the group G
109: replaced by a vertex algebra V . The difference between their bundle and ours is that in ours, not only is
110: the structure group G (whose action on the fibers is vertical ) replaced by a vertex algebra, but the fibers
111: themselves are represented in this vertex algebra. And still furthermore, the Lie algebra of vector fields
112: on M (whose action on the fibers is horizontal ) is part of our vertex algebra. This final aspect (chiralizing
113: 
114: 

137: souped-up Lie algebra we construct is the one associated to a Gvector bundle E with connection ∇,
138: discussed above in 1.1.1.
139:     In Chapter 4, we construct a vertex algebra from a souped-up Lie algebra. For some basic souped-
140: up Lie algebras s, we prove that the resulting vertex algebra contains s as a subspace. However, the
141: viability of this construction for a generic souped-up Lie algebra is ultimately a conjecture; the resulting
142: vertex algebra might be zero (and thus not a vertex algebra at all). That said, we are confident that the
143: resulting vertex algebra contains the entire generating souped-up Lie algebra. We prove some necessary
144: conditions for these conjectures to be true.
145:     The importance of Chapter 5 is the definition of the functor V from souped-up Lie algebras to vertex
146: algebras. This functor will be used to associate a sheaf of souped-up Lie algebras to a sheaf of vertex
147: algebras.
148:     Next, in Chapter 6, we construct a sheaf of vertex algebras from a sheaf of souped-up Lie algebras.
149:     Finally, in Chapter 7, we apply this construction to the sheaf of souped-up Lie algebras associated to
150: (E, ∇), giving us a sheaf of vertex algebras containing all the geometric and algebraic information from
151: (E, ∇).
152:

153: 1.2.1    A note on superspaces and supercommutativity
154: Throughout this manuscript, we will assume that every vector space is in fact a superspace, meaning it
155: has a Z2 -grading. Of course, it is certainly possible that the odd component is trivial. Furthermore, we
156: will use commutative to mean supercommutative. Thus a commutative ring has a Z2 -grading in which
157: odd elements x, y satisfy xy +yx = 0. Similarly, in an abelian Lie algebra g, the associative multiplication

780: contains s as a subalgebra, with the bracket [·, ·] played by ◦0 and the scalar multiplication by a played
781: by ◦−1 . We know this to be true in two basic cases: when g = 0, in which case s(0, a) ∼ = a (Theorem 4.4);
782: and s(g, C), where C is the trivial representation for g. In this case, s is really just a central extension
783: of g by the ring of coefficients C (Theorem 4.6). However, for a general souped-up Lie algebra s, it is
784: only conjectured that s survives intact in V (s) (Conjecture 4.7). We will discuss compelling reasons for
785: this conjecture below.
786:     First we state and prove the two known cases.
787: Theorem 4.4. The map taking the souped-up Lie algebra s(0, a) ∼     = a to its image in V (s) = F (s)/I(s),
788: with N (s, t) ≡ 0, is injective. Equivalently, I(s) ∩ s = {0}.
789:     This proof would require very little effort if every generator of I(s) had homogeneous length at least
790: 2. In that case, the length decomposition of F (s) would descend onto V (s), and then it would suffice to
791: note that s and the ideal I(s) intersect trivially since the former space in the length 1 summand, while
792: I(s) is orthogonal to that. Thus nothing in s is killed in the quotient F (s)/I(s).
793:     Alas, this is not the case, since the generators a[[a, s]] ≡ a−1 s − as do not have homogeneous length.
794: (All other generators do have homogeneous length, including s[[s, t]] ≡ s0 t − [s, t], since [s, t] = 0 in this
795: setup.)
796: 
797: Proof of Theorem 4.4. We will construct a homomorphism R (of vector spaces, not of infinite free alge-

781: by ◦−1 . We know this to be true in two basic cases: when g = 0, in which case s(0, a) ∼ = a (Theorem 4.4);
782: and s(g, C), where C is the trivial representation for g. In this case, s is really just a central extension
783: of g by the ring of coefficients C (Theorem 4.6). However, for a general souped-up Lie algebra s, it is
784: only conjectured that s survives intact in V (s) (Conjecture 4.7). We will discuss compelling reasons for
785: this conjecture below.
786:     First we state and prove the two known cases.
787: Theorem 4.4. The map taking the souped-up Lie algebra s(0, a) ∼     = a to its image in V (s) = F (s)/I(s),
788: with N (s, t) ≡ 0, is injective. Equivalently, I(s) ∩ s = {0}.
789:     This proof would require very little effort if every generator of I(s) had homogeneous length at least
790: 2. In that case, the length decomposition of F (s) would descend onto V (s), and then it would suffice to
791: note that s and the ideal I(s) intersect trivially since the former space in the length 1 summand, while
792: I(s) is orthogonal to that. Thus nothing in s is killed in the quotient F (s)/I(s).
793:     Alas, this is not the case, since the generators a[[a, s]] ≡ a−1 s − as do not have homogeneous length.
794: (All other generators do have homogeneous length, including s[[s, t]] ≡ s0 t − [s, t], since [s, t] = 0 in this
795: setup.)
796: 
797: Proof of Theorem 4.4. We will construct a homomorphism R (of vector spaces, not of infinite free alge-
798: bras) from F (s) to itself that fixes every element of s and projects I(s) onto the orthogonal complement

866: is precisely the ideal of relations in the well-known current algebra construction ([Kac97], [LL07]) with
867: vanishing bilinear form B on g. In a current algebra, it is known that the entire centrally extended
868: generating Lie algebra survives intact in the quotient V (s), and the theorem is proved.
869: 
870:     These two theorems leads us to conjecture that this injectivity holds for an arbitrary souped-up Lie
871: algebra.
872: Conjecture 4.7. There exists some constant N such that, for any souped-up Lie algebra s, we have
873: I(s) ∩ s = {0} with N (s, t) ≡ N .
874:    For the remainder of this paper, we will assume Conjecture 4.7. The following two propositions are
875: necessary conditions that are implied by this conjecture.
876: Proposition 4.8. In general, even if s is regarded as a two-sided module for a with the relation as = sa,
877: the set of elements a[[a, s]] ≡ a−1 s − as ∈ I(s) may not be enlarged to include the elements s−1 a − as.
878:     There are cases where such an addition would not affect the vertex algebra V (s) adversely. These
879: include the two examples in Theorems 4.4 and 4.6. But there are important cases in which adding the
880: generators corresponding to a right multiplication by a actually collapses V (s) to 0

=== 1004.3332 ===

1:                                         Estimation in Gaussian Noise: Properties of the
2:                                                  Minimum Mean-Square Error
3:                                                       Dongning Guo, Yihong Wu, Shlomo Shamai (Shitz), and Sergio Verdú
4: 
5: 
6: 
7:                                                                                                  Abstract
8:                                                   Consider the minimum mean-square error (MMSE) of estimating an arbitrary random variable from
9:                                              its observation contaminated by Gaussian noise. The MMSE can be regarded as a function of the signal-
10: 
11: 
12: 
13: 
14: arXiv:1004.3332v1 [cs.IT] 20 Apr 2010
15:                                              to-noise ratio (SNR) as well as a functional of the input distribution (of the random variable to be
16:                                              estimated). It is shown that the MMSE is concave in the input distribution at any given SNR. For a
17:                                              given input distribution, the MMSE is found to be infinitely differentiable at all positive SNR, and in
18:                                              fact a real analytic function in SNR under mild conditions. The key to these regularity results is that the

1112: of the input distribution and function of the SNR. Because of the intimate relationship MMSE has with
1113: information measures, its properties find direct use in a number of problems in information theory.
1114:    The MMSE can be viewed as a transform from the input distribution to a function of the SNR:
1115: PX 7→ {mmse(PX , γ), γ ∈ [0, ∞)}. An interesting question remains to be answered: Is this transform
1116: one-to-one? We have the following conjecture:
1117:    Conjecture 1: For any zero-mean random variables X and Z , mmse(X, snr) ≡ mmse(Z, snr) for all
1118: snr ∈ [0, ∞) if and only if X is identically distributed as either Z or −Z .
1119:    There is an intimate relationship between the real analyticity of MMSE and Conjecture 1. In particular,
1120: MMSE being real-analytic at zero SNR for all input and MMSE being an injective transform on the set of
1121: all random variables (with shift and reflection identified) cannot both hold. This is because given the real
1122: analyticity at zero SNR, MMSE can be extended to an open disk D centered at zero via the power series
1123: expansion, where the coefficients depend only on the moments of X . Since solution to the Hamburger
1124: moment problem is not unique in general, there may exist different X and X 0 with the same moments, and
1125: hence their MMSE function coincide in D. By the identity theorem of analytic functions, they coincide
1126: everywhere, hence on the real line. Nonetheless, if one is restricted to the class of sub-Gaussian random
1127: variables, the moments determine the distribution uniquely by Carleman’s condition [24].
1128: 
1129:                                               A PPENDIX A

=== 1005.3133 ===

1:                                            TROESCH COMPLEXES AND EXTENSIONS OF STRICT
2:                                                      POLYNOMIAL FUNCTORS
3: 
4:                                                                              ANTOINE TOUZÉ
5: 
6: 
7:                                                   Abstract. We develop a new approach of extension calculus in the
8: 
9: 
10: 
11: 
12: arXiv:1005.3133v3 [math.RT] 26 May 2011
13:                                                   category of strict polynomial functors, based on Troesch complexes.
14:                                                   We obtain new short elementary proofs of numerous classical Ext-
15:                                                   computations as well as new results.
16:                                                      In particular, we get a cohomological version of the ‘fundamental
17:                                                   theorems’ from classical invariant invariant theory for GLn for n big
18:                                                   enough (and we give a conjecture for smaller values of n).

14:                                                   We obtain new short elementary proofs of numerous classical Ext-
15:                                                   computations as well as new results.
16:                                                      In particular, we get a cohomological version of the ‘fundamental
17:                                                   theorems’ from classical invariant invariant theory for GLn for n big
18:                                                   enough (and we give a conjecture for smaller values of n).
19:                                                      We also study the ‘twisting spectral sequence’ E s,t (F, G, r) converg-
20:                                                   ing to the extension groups Ext∗Pk (F (r) , G(r) ) between the twisted func-
21:                                                   tors F (r) and G(r) . Many classical Ext computations simply amount to
22:                                                   the collapsing of this spectral sequence at the second page (for lacunary
23:                                                   reasons), and it is also a convenient tool to study the eﬀect of the Frobe-
24:                                                   nius twist on Ext groups. We prove many cases of collapsing, and we
25:                                                   conjecture collapsing is a general fact.
26: 
27: 
28: 
29: 
30:                                                                             1. Introduction
31:                                              Let k be a ﬁeld of prime characteristic p. In [FS], Friedlander and Suslin

21:                                                   tors F (r) and G(r) . Many classical Ext computations simply amount to
22:                                                   the collapsing of this spectral sequence at the second page (for lacunary
23:                                                   reasons), and it is also a convenient tool to study the eﬀect of the Frobe-
24:                                                   nius twist on Ext groups. We prove many cases of collapsing, and we
25:                                                   conjecture collapsing is a general fact.
26: 
27: 
28: 
29: 
30:                                                                             1. Introduction
31:                                              Let k be a ﬁeld of prime characteristic p. In [FS], Friedlander and Suslin
32:                                           introduced the category Pk of strict polynomial functors (of ﬁnite type) over
33:                                           k. Let Vk the category of ﬁnite dimensional k-vector spaces. Roughly speak-
34:                                           ing, objects of Pk are functors F : Vk → Vk with some additional polynomial
35:                                           structure, so that the following property holds. If G is an algebraic group
36:                                           (or group scheme) over k acting rationally on V , then functoriality deﬁnes
37:                                           a rational action of G on F (V ). Such functors occur very frequently in
38:                                           representation theory. Typical examples are symmetric powers S n , tensor

103:        classical invariant theory. We give a conjecture for smaller n.

119:    Let us review more speciﬁcally the content of the paper. Sections 2 and 3
120: are mainly expository. They collect well-know facts about Pk and describe
121: the properties of Troesch coresolutions which we need for our computations
122: (in propositions 3.4 and 3.7). For our computations, we do not need an
123: explicit description of the diﬀerentials of Troesch coresolutions. However, for
124: the reader’s convenience, we have recalled their construction in an appendix.
125:    In section 4, we present our elementary proof of Chalupnik’s isomorphism
126: (∗). As corollaries, we retrieve many computations from [FS, FFSS, C1]. Be-

146: this with the previous computations of section 5 we prove a cohomologi-
147: cal analogue of the ‘Fundamental Theorems’ from classical invariant the-
148: ory. Namely, we describe the cohomology algebra H ∗ (GLn , S ∗ ((kn (r) )⊕k ⊕
149: (kn (r) )∨ ⊕ℓ ). Actually our result is valid for n ≥ pr min{k, ℓ}. For n smaller,
150: we state a conjecture.
151:     We introduce the ‘twisting spectral sequence’ E s,t (F, G, r) in section 7.
152: The second page of this spectral sequence is given by extension groups be-
153: tween F and G precomposed by the functor V 7→ Er ⊗ V , and it converges
154: to the extension groups Ext∗Pk (F ◦ I (r) , G ◦ I (r) ). Although the construction
155: of the twisting spectral sequence is a formal consequence of Chalupnik’s
156: isomorphism, it is an interesting tool to study extensions between twisted
157: functors. Indeed, the eﬀect of cup products and the eﬀect of the twisting
158: map Fr1 may be easily read on the second page, so the twisting spectral
159: sequence is a convenient way to study them. For example, we can read the
160: ‘twist stability’ phenomenon [FFSS, Cor 4.10] on the second page, and the
161: 
162: 4                                ANTOINE TOUZÉ
163: 

166: ond page. Also, many classical computations amount to the collapsing (for
167: lacunary reasons) of the twisting spectral sequence at the second page.
168:    In fact, we observe that the twisting spectral sequence collapses at the
169: second page in all the computations known so far, even when there is no
170: lacunary reason for it, and we conjecture this is a general fact. As a main
171: result, we make a step towards this conjecture, by proving in section 8 that
172: E(F, G, r) collapses at the second page for all r ≥ 0 and many pairs (F, G),
173: including all pairs (F, G) studied in [FS, FFSS, C1, C2]. We also propose
174: to the reader a combinatorial problem whose positive solution would prove
175: the collapsing for any F, G.
176: 
177:                       2. Background and notations
178:    In the article, we assume from the reader only a basic knowledge of the
179: category Pk , corresponding to section 2 of the seminal article [FS]. We do not
180: assume that any Ext-computation is known (we redo all the computations
181: from scratch). In this background section, we introduce notations (most of
182: them are standard), and we write down a few useful facts which are either
183: implicit in, or easy consequences of [FS, Section 2].

18:                                                                                       1. Introduction
19:                                             The classical Laguerre inequality for polynomials states that a polynomial of degree n
20:                                          with only real zeros, p(x) ∈ R[x], satisfies (n − 1)p′ (x)2 − np′′ (x)p(x) ≥ 0 for all x ∈ R (see
21:                                          [3, 13]). Thus, the classical Laguerre inequality is a necessary condition for a polynomial
22:                                          to have only real zeros. Our investigation is inspired by an interesting paper of I. Krasikov
23:                                          [8]. He proves several discrete polynomial inequalities, including useful versions of gen-
24:                                          eralized Laguerre inequalities [17], and shows how to apply them by obtaining bounds on

21:                                          [3, 13]). Thus, the classical Laguerre inequality is a necessary condition for a polynomial
22:                                          to have only real zeros. Our investigation is inspired by an interesting paper of I. Krasikov
23:                                          [8]. He proves several discrete polynomial inequalities, including useful versions of gen-
24:                                          eralized Laguerre inequalities [17], and shows how to apply them by obtaining bounds on
25:                                          the zeros of some Krawtchouk polynomials. In [8], I. Krasikov conjectures a new discrete
26:                                          Laguerre inequality for polynomials. After establishing this conjecture, we generalize the
27:                                          inequality to transcendental entire functions (of order ρ < 2, and minimal type of order
28:                                          ρ = 2) in the Laguerre-Pólya class (see Definition 1.1).

114: 

121: attention on transportation polytopes, which are sets of all tables of non-negative real
122: numbers satisfying certain summation conditions. Transportation problems are, in many
123: ways, the simplest kind of linear programs and thus have a rich combinatorial structure. First,
124: we give new results on the diameters of certain classes of transportation polytopes and their
125: relation to the Hirsch Conjecture, which asserts that the diameter of every d-dimensional
126: convex polytope with n facets is bounded above by n − d. In particular, we prove a new
127: quadratic upper bound on the diameter of 3-way axial transportation polytopes defined by
128: 1-marginals. We also show that the Hirsch Conjecture holds for p × 2 classical transportation
129: polytopes, but that there are infinitely-many Hirsch-sharp classical transportation polytopes.
130:     Second, we present new results on subpolytopes of transportation polytopes. We
131: investigate, for example, a non-regular triangulation of a subpolytope of the fourth Birkhoff
132: polytope B4 . This implies the existence of non-regular triangulations of all Birkhoff polytopes
133: Bn for n ≥ 4. We also study certain classes of network flow polytopes and prove new linear
134: upper bounds for their diameters.
135:     The thesis is organized as follows: Chapter 1 introduces polytopes and polyhedra and
136: discusses their connection to optimization. A survey on transportation polytopes is presented
137: here. We close the chapter by discussing the theory behind the software transportgen.
138: Chapter 2 surveys the state of the art on the Hirsch Conjecture and its variants. Chapter 3

124: we give new results on the diameters of certain classes of transportation polytopes and their
125: relation to the Hirsch Conjecture, which asserts that the diameter of every d-dimensional
126: convex polytope with n facets is bounded above by n − d. In particular, we prove a new
127: quadratic upper bound on the diameter of 3-way axial transportation polytopes defined by
128: 1-marginals. We also show that the Hirsch Conjecture holds for p × 2 classical transportation
129: polytopes, but that there are infinitely-many Hirsch-sharp classical transportation polytopes.
130:     Second, we present new results on subpolytopes of transportation polytopes. We
131: investigate, for example, a non-regular triangulation of a subpolytope of the fourth Birkhoff
132: polytope B4 . This implies the existence of non-regular triangulations of all Birkhoff polytopes
133: Bn for n ≥ 4. We also study certain classes of network flow polytopes and prove new linear
134: upper bounds for their diameters.
135:     The thesis is organized as follows: Chapter 1 introduces polytopes and polyhedra and
136: discusses their connection to optimization. A survey on transportation polytopes is presented
137: here. We close the chapter by discussing the theory behind the software transportgen.
138: Chapter 2 surveys the state of the art on the Hirsch Conjecture and its variants. Chapter 3
139: presents our new results on the geometric combinatorics of transportation polytopes. Finally,
140: a summary of the computational results of the software package transportgen are presented
141: in Appendix A.

13:                                                     In 1941, Turán conjectured that the edge density of any 3-graph
14:                                                 without independent sets on 4 vertices (Turán (3, 4)-graph) is ≥ 4/9(1−
15:                                                 o(1)), and he gave the first example witnessing this bound. Brown
16:                                                 (1983) and Kostochka (1982) found many other examples of this den-
17:                                                 sity. Fon-der-Flaass (1988) presented a general construction that con-
18:                                                 verts an arbitrary C ~ 4 -free orgraph Γ into a Turán (3, 4)-graph. He
19:                                                 observed that all Turán-Brown-Kostochka examples result from his
20:                                                 construction, and proved the bound ≥ 3/7(1 − o(1)) on the edge den-
21:                                                 sity of any Turán (3, 4)-graph obtainable in this way.

101:     One prominent way to attack any extremal problem is by better under-
102: standing the structure of its (conjectured) extremal configurations. And one
103: of the (many) difficulties associated with Turán’s (3, 4)-problem is that this
104: set is extraordinarily complex and consists of a continuous family of princi-
105: pally different configurations (in terminology of [Raz07], a family of different
106: homomorphisms φ ∈ Hom+ (A0 , R) attaining φ(ρ3 ) = 4/9) parameterized by
107: probability distributions on the real line. These examples were constructed
108: by Brown [Bro83] and Kostochka [Kos82], and the only (to the best of our
109: knowledge) successful attempt to “explain” them in “external” terms was
110: undertaken by Fon-der-Flaass [FdF88].

134:    While we have not been able to solve it completely, our main result (The-
135: orem 2.3) achieves this goal under any one of the following two assumptions:
136:

138:       anced and with more than three parts);
139: 
140:    2. the edge density of Γ is ≥ 2/3 − ǫ, where ǫ > 0 is an absolute constant.

9:                                                  Abstract. A graph homomorphism from the rooted d-branching tree φ :
10:                                                  T d → H is said to be cold if the values of φ for vertices arbitrarily far away from
11:                                                  the root can restrict the value of φ at the root. Warmth is a graph parameter
12:                                                  that measures the non-existence of cold maps. We study warmth of random
13:                                                  graphs G(n, p), and for every d ≥ 1, we exhibit a nearly-sharp threshold for
14:                                                  the existence of cold maps. As a corollary, for p = O(n−α ) warmth of G(n, p)
15:                                                  is concentrated on at most two values. As another corollary, a conjecture of
16:                                                  Lovász relating mobility to chromatic number holds for “almost all” graphs.
17:                                                  Finally, our results suggest new conjectures relating graph parameters from
18:                                                  statistical physics with graph parameters from equivariant topology.

16:                                                  Lovász relating mobility to chromatic number holds for “almost all” graphs.

18:                                                  statistical physics with graph parameters from equivariant topology.

22:                                                                                1. Introduction
23:                                             Graph homomorphisms provide a natural combinatorial framework for statistical
24:                                          physics. In combinatorial statistical physics, one is often interested in an infinite
25:                                          graph G such as a lattice or a tree, representing a “ground space”, and then a finite
26:                                          target graph H, representing physical “constraints”. Then graph homomorphisms
27:                                          φ : G → H correspond to allowable states of the system. See Winkler’s ICM notes
28:                                          [BW02] for an introduction and overview.

23:                                             Graph homomorphisms provide a natural combinatorial framework for statistical
24:                                          physics. In combinatorial statistical physics, one is often interested in an infinite
25:                                          graph G such as a lattice or a tree, representing a “ground space”, and then a finite
26:                                          target graph H, representing physical “constraints”. Then graph homomorphisms
27:                                          φ : G → H correspond to allowable states of the system. See Winkler’s ICM notes
28:                                          [BW02] for an introduction and overview.
29:                                             Brightwell and Winkler studied specifically maps from the rooted d-branching
30:                                          tree G = T d to finite and loopless targets ([BW04]). Motivated by statistical

40:                                          are illustrated in Figure 1. As a corollary of our results, warmth is concentrated at
41:                                          most at two values, as long as p = O (n−α ). This is perhaps surprising, as warmth
42:                                          is not a monotone property.
43:                                             Brightwell and Winkler proved a number of results relating statistical physics
44:                                          with graph coloring. As one corollary of our main results, we verify that a conjecture
45:                                          of Lovász that mobility (defined in Section 6) is a lower bound on chromatic number
46:                                          holds “almost always,” in the sense that it holds for random graphs with high
47:                                          probability.

12:                                                   with continuous piecewise linear coefficients are considered. The main
13:                                                   concern is to find the maximum possible multiplicity of periodic solu-

15:                                                   the coefficients are polynomial functions of degree n, or piecewise linear
16:                                                   functions with n segments.

1:                                                           ON A COMBINATORIAL PROBLEM OF
2:                                                             ERDŐS, KLEITMAN AND LEMKE
3: 
4:                                                                                              by
5: 
6:                                                                                   Benjamin Girard
7: 
8: 
9: 
10: 
11: arXiv:1010.5042v2 [math.CO] 12 Aug 2012
12:                                                 Abstract. — In this paper, we study a combinatorial problem originating in the following
13:                                                 conjecture of Erdős and Lemke: given any sequence of n divisors of n, repetitions being
14:                                                 allowed, there exists a subsequence the elements of which are summing to n. This conjecture
15:                                                 was proved by Kleitman and Lemke, who then extended the original question to a problem
16:                                                 on a zero-sum invariant in the framework of finite Abelian groups. Building among others
17:                                                 on earlier works by Alon and Dubiner and by the author, our main theorem gives a new
18:                                                 upper bound for this invariant in the general case, and provides its right order of magnitude.

9:

11: arXiv:1010.5042v2 [math.CO] 12 Aug 2012
12:                                                 Abstract. — In this paper, we study a combinatorial problem originating in the following
13:                                                 conjecture of Erdős and Lemke: given any sequence of n divisors of n, repetitions being
14:                                                 allowed, there exists a subsequence the elements of which are summing to n. This conjecture
15:                                                 was proved by Kleitman and Lemke, who then extended the original question to a problem
16:                                                 on a zero-sum invariant in the framework of finite Abelian groups. Building among others
17:                                                 on earlier works by Alon and Dubiner and by the author, our main theorem gives a new
18:                                                 upper bound for this invariant in the general case, and provides its right order of magnitude.

21:                                             Let G be a finite Abelian group, written additively. If G is cyclic of order n, it will be
22:                                           denoted by Cn . In the general case, we can decompose G as a direct product of cyclic
23:                                           groups Cn1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Cnr such that 1 < n1 | · · · | nr ∈ N, where r and nr are respectively
24:                                           called the rank and exponent of G. Usually, the exponent of G is simply denoted by
25:                                           exp(G). The order of an element g of G will be written ord(g) and for every divisor d of
26:                                           exp(G), we denote by Gd the subgroup of G consisting of all elements of order dividing d:

22:                                           denoted by Cn . In the general case, we can decompose G as a direct product of cyclic
23:                                           groups Cn1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Cnr such that 1 < n1 | · · · | nr ∈ N, where r and nr are respectively
24:                                           called the rank and exponent of G. Usually, the exponent of G is simply denoted by
25:                                           exp(G). The order of an element g of G will be written ord(g) and for every divisor d of
26:                                           exp(G), we denote by Gd the subgroup of G consisting of all elements of order dividing d:
27:                                                                                 Gd = {x ∈ G | dx = 0} .

49:    The investigations on t(G) originate in the following conjecture, addressed by Erdős
50: and Lemke in the late eighties (see [15, Introduction]). Is it true that out of n divisors
51: of n, repetitions being allowed, one can always find a certain number of them that sum
52: up to n? Motivated by this conjecture, Kleitman and Lemke [15, Theorem 1] proved the
53: following stronger result.
54: Theorem 1.1 (Kleitman and Lemke [15]). — For any given integers a1 , . . . , an
55: there is a non-empty subset I ⊆ J1, nK such that
56:                               X            X
57:                           n|     ai and        gcd(ai , n) ≤ n.
58:                                 i∈I            i∈I
59: 
60:    Meanwhile, Lagarias and Saks framed a graph-theoretic approach, called graph peb-
61: bling, thanks to which the problem of Erdős and Lemke could be reduced to the study
62: of a combinatorial game, played with pebbles on the vertices of a simple graph. In this
63: context, Chung [2, Theorem 6] found a new elegant proof of Theorem 1.1, and, under one
64: extra assumption made on the prime factors of n, Denley [3, Theorem 1] could sharpen
65: this theorem. Graph pebbling led to a rapidly growing literature as well as many open

66: problems. The interested reader is referred to the surveys [13, 14] which contain many
67: references on the subject.
68:    In addition, let us underline that, with our notation, Theorem 1.1 simply asserts that
69: t(Cn ) ≤ n, and this upper bound is easily seen to be optimal. In the general case, Kleitman
70: and Lemke [15, Section 3] conjectured that t(G) ≤ |G| holds for every finite Abelian group
71: G. This conjecture was proved, using tools from zero-sum theory, by Geroldinger [8]. An
72: alternative proof was then found by Elledge and Hurlbert [5, Theorem 2] using graph
73: pebbling.
74:   In this paper, we prove that in the general case of finite Abelian groups, the currently
75: known upper bound on t(G) can be improved significantly. More precisely, our main
76: theorem shows that, for finite Abelian groups of fixed rank, t(G) grows linearly in the
77: exponent of G, which gives the correct order of magnitude. To do so, we prove that t(G)
78: can be bounded above using a result of Alon and Dubiner [1] on the following classical
79: invariant in zero-sum theory.
80:    By η(G) we denote the smallest integer t ∈ N∗ such that every sequence S over G of
81: length |S| ≥ t contains a non-empty zero-sum subsequence S ′ | S with |S ′ | ≤ exp(G).
82: Such a subsequence is called a short zero-sum subsequence.
83:   Since k(T ) ≤ 1 implies |T | ≤ exp(G), one can notice that η(G) ≤ t(G) always holds.

14:                                                 ifications and relaxations of Sendov’s conjecture about the location of critical

1:                                                              THE NUMBER OF SOLUTIONS OF λ(x) = n
2: 
3:                                                                          KEVIN FORD AND FLORIAN LUCA
4: 
5:                                                 Abstract. We study the question of whether for each n there is an m 6= n with λ(m) =
6: 
7: 
8: 
9: 
10: arXiv:1010.5442v2 [math.NT] 3 Apr 2011
11:                                                 λ(n), where λ is Carmichael’s function. We give a “near” proof of the fact that this is
12:                                                 the case unconditionally, and a complete conditional proof under the Extended Riemann
13:                                                 Hypothesis.

16:                                                                To Professor Carl Pomerance on his 65th birthday

16:                                                                To Professor Carl Pomerance on his 65th birthday

18:                                                                                  1. Introduction
19:                                            Let λ(n) be the Carmichael function, that is, λ(n) is the largest order of any number
20:                                          modulo n. Recently, Banks et al [1] made the following conjecture:
21:                                          Conjecture 1. For every positive integer n, there is an integer m 6= n with λ(m) = λ(n).
22:                                              The analogous question for the Euler function φ(n) is known as Carmichael’s conjecture
23:                                          and remains unsolved. If there are counterexamples to Conjecture 1, the authors of [1] proved
24:                                          that all such counterexamples n are multiples of the smallest counterexample n0 . Further,
25:                                          they showed that if n0 exists, then n0 is divisible by every prime less than 30000. In this
26:                                          note, we prove that Conjecture 1 follows from the Extended Riemann Hypothesis (ERH) for
27:                                          Dirichlet L-functions, and also we come very close to proving the conjecture unconditionally.
28:                                              If n has prime factorization n = pe11 · · · pekk , then λ(n) = [λ(pe11 ), . . . , λ(pekk )], where
29:                                          [a1 , . . . , ak ] denotes the least common multiple of a1 , . . . , ak , λ(pe ) = pe−1 (p − 1) when p
30:                                          is odd or e ≤ 2, and λ(2e ) = 2e−2 when e ≥ 3. The following is proved in §7 of [1].
31:                                          Lemma 1.1. Suppose n0 exists, that is, Conjecture 1 is false. Then (i) 24 |n0 and (ii) if
32:                                          (p − 1)|λ(n0 ) for a prime p, then p2 |n0 .
33:                                          Proof. Since λ(1) = λ(2) and λ(4) = λ(8), part (i) follows. If (p − 1)|λ(n0 ) and p ∤ n0 ,

1:                                              A NOTE ON SOME EMBEDDING PROBLEMS FOR ORIENTED
2:                                                                  GRAPHS

7: arXiv:1011.4476v1 [math.CO] 19 Nov 2010
8:                                                                                 ANDREW TREGLOWN
9: 
10: 
11:                                                   Abstract. We conjecture that every oriented graph G on n vertices with δ + (G), δ − (G) ≥
12:                                                   5n/12 contains the square of a Hamilton cycle. We also give a conjectural bound on
13:                                                   the minimum semidegree which ensures a perfect packing of transitive triangles in an
14:                                                   oriented graph. A link between Ramsey numbers and perfect packings of transitive
15:                                                   tournaments is also considered.

9: 
10: 
11:                                                   Abstract. We conjecture that every oriented graph G on n vertices with δ + (G), δ − (G) ≥
12:                                                   5n/12 contains the square of a Hamilton cycle. We also give a conjectural bound on
13:                                                   the minimum semidegree which ensures a perfect packing of transitive triangles in an
14:                                                   oriented graph. A link between Ramsey numbers and perfect packings of transitive
15:                                                   tournaments is also considered.
16: 
17: 
18: 
19:                                           1.1. Powers of Hamilton cycles. One of the most studied problems in graph theory
20:                                           concerns finding sufficient conditions that ensure a graph contains a Hamilton cycle.
21:                                           Dirac [4] showed that any graph G on n ≥ 3 vertices has a Hamilton cycle provided
22:                                           that it has minimum degree δ(G) at least n/2. For a digraph G it is natural to consider
23:                                           its minimum semidegree δ0 (G), which is the minimum of its minimum outdegree δ+ (G)
24:                                           and its minimum indegree δ− (G). (The digraphs we consider do not have loops and we
25:                                           allow at most one edge in each direction between any pair of vertices.) Ghouila-Houri [6]

26:                                           proved that every digraph G on n ≥ 2 vertices with δ0 (G) ≥ n/2 is Hamiltonian.
27:                                              An important subclass of digraphs is the class of oriented graphs: these are the digraphs
28:                                           which do not contain any 2-cycles. Keevash, Kühn and Osthus [9] showed that any
29:                                           sufficiently large oriented graph G on n vertices with δ0 (G) ≥ (3n − 4)/8 is Hamiltonian,
30:                                           thereby proving a conjecture of Häggkvist [7]. For a detailed account of other such results
31:                                           concerning Hamilton cycles in directed and oriented graphs see [13].
32:                                              A generalisation of the notion of a Hamilton cycle is that of the rth power of a Hamilton
33:                                           cycle. Indeed, the rth power of a Hamilton cycle C is obtained from C by adding an edge
34:                                           between every pair of vertices of distance at most r on C. Seymour [18] conjectured the
35:                                           following strengthening of Dirac’s theorem.
36:                                                                                                                   r
37:                                           Conjecture 1 (Seymour [18]). Let G be a graph on n vertices. If δ(G) ≥ r+1 n then G
38:                                           contains the rth power of a Hamilton cycle.
39:                                              Pósa (see [5]) had earlier proposed the conjecture in the case of the square of a Hamilton
40:                                           cycle (that is, when r = 2). Komlós, Sárközy and Szemerédi [11] proved Conjecture 1 for
41:                                           sufficiently large graphs.
42:                                              The notion of the rth power of a Hamilton cycle also makes sense in the digraph
43:                                           setting: In this case the rth power of a Hamilton cycle C is the digraph obtained from

30:                                           thereby proving a conjecture of Häggkvist [7]. For a detailed account of other such results
31:                                           concerning Hamilton cycles in directed and oriented graphs see [13].
32:                                              A generalisation of the notion of a Hamilton cycle is that of the rth power of a Hamilton
33:                                           cycle. Indeed, the rth power of a Hamilton cycle C is obtained from C by adding an edge
34:                                           between every pair of vertices of distance at most r on C. Seymour [18] conjectured the
35:                                           following strengthening of Dirac’s theorem.
36:                                                                                                                   r
37:                                           Conjecture 1 (Seymour [18]). Let G be a graph on n vertices. If δ(G) ≥ r+1 n then G
38:                                           contains the rth power of a Hamilton cycle.
39:                                              Pósa (see [5]) had earlier proposed the conjecture in the case of the square of a Hamilton
40:                                           cycle (that is, when r = 2). Komlós, Sárközy and Szemerédi [11] proved Conjecture 1 for
41:                                           sufficiently large graphs.
42:                                              The notion of the rth power of a Hamilton cycle also makes sense in the digraph
43:                                           setting: In this case the rth power of a Hamilton cycle C is the digraph obtained from
44:                                           C by adding a directed edge from x to y if there is a path of length at most r from x
45:                                           to y on C. Bollobás and Häggkvist [1] proved that given any ε > 0 and any r ∈ N, all
46:                                           sufficiently large tournaments T on n vertices with δ0 (T ) ≥ (1/4 + ε)n contain the rth
47:                                           power of a Hamilton cycle.

1:                                                                      BOX-BASKET-BALL SYSTEMS
2: 
3:                                                         THOMAS LAM, PAVLO PYLYAVSKYY, AND REIHO SAKAMOTO
4: 
5:                                                  Abstract. Using the whurl relation of the first two authors, we define a new discrete
6: 
7: 
8: 
9: 
10: arXiv:1011.5930v2 [math.QA] 26 Jun 2012
11:                                                  solitonic system, which we call the box-basket-ball system, generalizing the box-ball
12:                                                  system of Takahashi and Satsuma. In box-basket-ball systems balls may be put either
13:                                                  into boxes or into baskets. While boxes stay fixed, both balls and baskets get moved
14:                                                  during time evolution. Balls and baskets behave as fermionic and bosonic particles
15:                                                  respectively. We classify the solitons of this system, and study their scattering.
16:

224: systems with balls of many different colors, or to systems with boxes with higher capacity.
225: As suggested by the model of networks on surfaces in [LP], one may consider a more
226: general setting of having wires in both directions. The action of the carrier in such
227: systems would be given by tropicalization of the corresponding whurl relations. We make
228: the following general conjecture.
229: Conjecture 2.4. The discrete dynamical systems arising from general whurl relations
230: exhibit solitonic behaviour.
231:   In this paper we shall consider the case of three horizontal wires, where two are oriented
232: to the right and one to the left, as shown in Figure 2. The whurl relation in this case is
233:

1:                                                      ZEROS OF THE BERGMAN KERNEL OF THE
2:                                                    FOCK-BARGMANN-HARTOGS DOMAIN AND THE
3:                                                             INTERLACING PROPERTY
4: 
5:

7: 
8: arXiv:1101.3135v1 [math.CV] 17 Jan 2011
9:                                                                            ATSUSHI YAMAMORI
10: 
11: 
12:                                                   Abstract. In this paper we consider the zeros of the Bergman kernel of the
13:                                                                                                                      2
14:                                                   Fock-Bargmann-Hartogs domain {(z, ζ) ∈ Cn × Cm ; ||ζ||2 < e−µ||z|| } where
15:                                                   µ > 0. We describe how the existence of zeros of the Bergman kernel depends
16:                                                   on the integers m and n with the help of the interlacing property.
17: 
18: 

19: 
20: 
21:                                                                              1. Introduction
22:                                              Let Ω be a domain in Cn and KΩ (z, w) its Bergman kernel. In [8], Lu Qi-Keng
23:                                           conjectured that if Ω is simply connected, then KΩ is zero-free on Ω × Ω. It is
24:                                           already known that this conjecture is false in general (see [1, 9]). A domain in Cn
25:                                           is called a Lu Qi-Keng domain if its Bergman kernel function is zero-free.
26:                                              Let µ > 0. In our previous works [10, 11], we obtained an explicit formula of
27:                                                                                                               2
28:                                           the Bergman kernel of Dn,m = {(z, ζ) ∈ Cn × Cm ; ||ζ||2 < e−µ||z|| } which is called
29:                                           the Fock-Bargmann-Hartogs domain (abbr. FBH domain) in this paper. The aim
30:                                           of this paper is to establish the following theorem:
31:                                           Theorem A. For any fixed n ∈ N, there exists a unique number m0 (n) ∈ N such
32:                                           that Dn,m is a Lu Qi-Keng domain if and only if m ≥ m0 (n).
33:                                             As a related work, we should mention the following result proved by L. Zhang
34:                                           and W. Yin in [12].
35:                                           Theorem B ([12, Theorem 1]). For fixed n and p, there exists a constant m0 =
36:                                           m0 (n, p) such that

=== 1101.5747 ===

1:                                                          Rainbow connections of graphs – A survey∗
2: 
3: 
4: 
5: 
6: arXiv:1101.5747v2 [math.CO] 1 Feb 2011
7:                                                                                   Xueliang Li, Yuefang Sun
8:                                                                         Center for Combinatorics and LPMC-TJKLC
9:                                                                        Nankai University, Tianjin 300071, P.R. China
10:                                                                       E-mails: lxl@nankai.edu.cn, syf@cfc.nankai.edu.cn
11: 
12: 
13: 
14: 
15:                                                                                            Abstract
16: 
17:                                                         The concept of rainbow connection was introduced by Chartrand et al. in 2008. It
18:                                                    is fairly interesting and recently quite a lot papers have been published about it. In this

19:                                                    survey we attempt to bring together most of the results and papers that dealt with it.
20:                                                    We begin with an introduction, and then try to organize the work into five categories,
21:                                                    including (strong) rainbow connection number, rainbow k-connectivity, k-rainbow in-
22:                                                    dex, rainbow vertex-connection number, algorithms and computational complexity.
23:                                                    This survey also contains some conjectures, open problems or questions.
24:                                                    Keywords: rainbow path, (strong) rainbow connection number, rainbow k-connectivity,
25:                                                    k-rainbow index, rainbow vertex-connection number, algorithm, computational com-
26:                                                    plexity
27:                                                    AMS Subject Classification 2000: 05C15, 05C40
28: 
29: 
30: 
31:                                          1         Introduction
32: 
33:                                          1.1        Motivation and definitions
34: 
35:                                              Connectivity is perhaps the most fundamental graph-theoretic subject, both in combi-
36:                                          natorial sense and the algorithmic sense. There are many elegant and powerful results on

281:     Recall the fact that if H is a connected spanning subgraph of a nontrivial (connected)

293: [10]. They showed the following example: see Figure 2.1, here G is obtained from H by
294: adding the edge e = uv, then H is a connected spanning subgraph of G. It is easy to show
295: that there is a strong rainbow coloring of H which costs six colors, but the graph G costs at
296: least seven colors to ensure its strong rainbow connection.

1:                                                     THE MÖBIUS FUNCTION OF SEPARABLE AND
2:                                                          DECOMPOSABLE PERMUTATIONS
3: 
4:                                                         ALEXANDER BURSTEIN, VÍT JELÍNEK, EVA JELÍNKOVÁ,
5:                                                                   AND EINAR STEINGRÍMSSON
6:

8: 
9: 
10: arXiv:1102.1611v1 [math.CO] 8 Feb 2011
11:                                                   Abstract. We give a recursive formula for the Möbius function of an interval
12:                                                   [σ, π] in the poset of permutations ordered by pattern containment in the case
13:                                                   where π is a decomposable permutation, that is, consists of two blocks where
14:                                                   the first one contains all the letters 1, 2, . . . , k for some k. This leads to many
15:                                                   special cases of more explicit formulas. It also gives rise to a computationally
16:                                                   efficient formula for the Möbius function in the case where σ and π are separa-
17:                                                   ble permutations. A permutation is separable if it can be generated from the
18:                                                   permutation 1 by successive sums and skew sums or, equivalently, if it avoids

79: previous results of Sagan and Vatter [5] for layered permutations.

81: we derive several bounds on the values of µ(σ, π) for σ and π separable. In [7],

83: pattern 132 (or any one of the patterns 213, 231, 312) the absolute value of the
84: Möbius function of the interval [σ, π] is bounded by the number of occurrences of σ
85: in π. We prove this conjecture for the more general class of separable permutations
86: (for arbitrary σ and π this bound does not hold in general). In particular, if

88: generalization of another conjecture mentioned in [7], showing that for any separable
89: permutation π, µ(1, π) is either 1, 0 or −1.

98: As another simple example, our results imply that when σ and π are permuta-
99: tions with decompositions σ = σ1 + σ2 and π = π1 + π2 , with π1 and π2 both
100: different from 1, then µ(σ, π) = µ(σ1 , π1 )µ(σ2 , π2 ) if π1 6= π2 , and µ(σ, π) =
101: µ(σ1 , π1 )µ(σ2 , π2 ) + µ(σ, π1 ) if π1 = π2 .

941: The next corollary confirms a (more general version of a) conjecture of Steingrı́msson
942: and Tenner [7].
943: Corollary 24. If π and σ are separable permutations, then |µ(σ, π)| is at most the
944: number of occurrences of σ in π.
945: 
946: Proof. This follows from the fact that the number of occurrences of σ in π is clearly
947: at least the number of normal embeddings of σ into π.                              

966: 
967:          5. Concluding remarks, conjectures and open problems
968: 
969: We have shown in Corollary 19 that µ(σ, π) can be computed efficiently when σ
970: and π are separable. The same argument does in fact apply in a more general
971: form: For any hereditary class C of permutations that is a closure of a finite set of
972: permutations, there is a polynomial-time algorithm to compute µ(σ, π) for a given
973: σ and π in C. We do not know whether such an algorithm also exists for more
974: general classes of permutations.
975: Bose, Buss and Lubiw [2] have shown that it is NP-hard for given permutations π
976: and σ to decide whether π contains σ. In view of this, it seems unlikely that µ(σ, π)
977: could be computed efficiently for general permutations σ and π.
978: Our results imply that for a separable permutation π, the Möbius function µ(1, π)
979: has absolute value at most 1. In fact, the class of separable permutations is the
980: unique largest hereditary class with this property, since any hereditary class not

15:                                                   We study a generalization of nonrepetitive sequences involving arith-

36:                                           proved, by a remarkable inductive construction, that there exist arbitrarily
37:                                           long nonrepetitive sequences over just three different symbols (see also [5],
38:                                           [4]). This discovery resulted in many unexpected applications inspiring a
39:                                           stream of research and leading to the emergence of new branches of math-
40:                                           ematics with a variety of challenging open problems (see [1], [3], [8], [11],
41:                                           [14]).
42:                                              One particular variant, proposed in [3], concerns nonrepetitive tilings,
43:                                           i.e., assignments of symbols to lattice points of the plane so that all lines in
44:                                           prescribed directions are nonrepetitive. This idea led Currie and Simpson
45:                                           [7] to consider sequences with a stronger property: all subsequences taken
46:                                           over arithmetic progressions of bounded common differences are nonrepet-
47:                                           itive. Let k ⩾ 1 be a fixed positive integer and let S(k) be the family of

75: original nonconstructive approach), we prove the result for a more general

319:    We would like to conclude the paper with a problem concerning infinite
320: sets of forbidden differences. Let K be a fixed (possibly infinite) set of posi-
321: tive integers. A coloring of the integers is K-nonrepetitive if every arithmetic
322: 
323:                               NONREPETITIVE SEQUENCES                                       7
324: 
325: 
326: progression with common difference in K forms a nonrepetitive sequence.
327: Denote by π(K) the minimum number of colors (possibly infinite) needed
328: for a K-nonrepetitive coloring of Z.
329:    A natural question is for which sets K the number π(K) is finite. An
330: obvious necessary condition is that the related integer distance graph (i.e.,
331: a graph on the set of vertices Z with two integers a > b joined by an

331: a graph on the set of vertices Z with two integers a > b joined by an
332: edge whenever their difference a − b is in K) has finite chromatic number,
333: denoted by χ(K). Theorem 2 shows that π(K) is finite for finite sets K.
334: More intriguing in this respect is the case of infinite sets K. We offer the
335: following conjecture in the spirit of Erdős.
336: Conjecture 2. π(K) is finite for every lacunary set K.
337:    A set K = {k1 < k2 < . . .} is lacunary if there is a real number δ > 0 such
338: that ki+1
339:        ki > 1 + δ for all indices i. For instance the set of powers of 2 and
340: the set of Fibonacci numbers are lacunary. It is known that for such sets
341: the usual chromatic number χ(K) is finite [13], [16], [17]. However, there
342: are non-lacunary sets with a finite chromatic number. Complete character-
343: ization of such sets is not known and, as pointed out by Ruzsa (personal
344: communication), this problem is connected to some deep questions in ad-
345: ditive number theory. A trivial example is the set of odd positive integers,
346: whose chromatic number is 2. Curiously, for the nonrepetitive variant just
347: 4 colors suffice as proved by Carpi [6], which supports even stronger suppo-
348: sition that perhaps π(K) is finite if and only if χ(K) is finite.

115: 1.2      Sparse Steiner systems
116: 
117: There are many problems and results concerning subfamilies of block designs, see, e.g., Colbourn
118: and Rosa [17]. A Steiner triple system STS(n) := S(n, 3, 2) is called e-sparse if it contains no set of e
119: distinct triples spanning at most e+2 points. Every Steiner triple system is 3-sparse. A longstanding
120: conjecture of Erdős [22] is that for every e ≥ 4 there exists an n0 (e) such that if n > n0 (e) and n
121: is admissible (i.e., n ≡ 1 or 3 (mod 6)), then there exists an e-sparse STS(n). Systems that are

116: 
117: There are many problems and results concerning subfamilies of block designs, see, e.g., Colbourn
118: and Rosa [17]. A Steiner triple system STS(n) := S(n, 3, 2) is called e-sparse if it contains no set of e
119: distinct triples spanning at most e+2 points. Every Steiner triple system is 3-sparse. A longstanding
120: conjecture of Erdős [22] is that for every e ≥ 4 there exists an n0 (e) such that if n > n0 (e) and n
121: is admissible (i.e., n ≡ 1 or 3 (mod 6)), then there exists an e-sparse STS(n). Systems that are
122: 4-sparse are those without a Pasch configuration (4 blocks spanning 6 points, P4 , {a, b, c}, {a, d, e},
123: {b, d, f }, {c, e, f }). Completing the works of Brouwer [10], Murphy [41, 42], Ling and Colbourn [53]
124: and others, finally Grannell, Griggs and Whitehead [40] proved that 4-sparse STS(n)’s exist for all
125: admissible n except 7 and 13.
126:    A 5-sparse system is precisely one lacking Pasch, P4 , and mitre configurations, M5 , the latter
127: comprising five blocks of the form {a, b, c}, {a, d, e}, {a, f, g}, {b, d, f }, {c, e, g}. In a sequence of
128: papers (e.g., Colbourn, Mendelsohn, Rosa, and Širáň [16]) culminating in Y. Fujiwara [36] and
129: Wolfe [69] it was established that systems having no mitres exist for all admissible orders, except for
130: n = 9.
131:    Concerning the even more difficult problem of constructing 5-sparse systems (see Ling [54])
132: Wolfe [68, 70] proved that such systems exist for almost all admissible n. More precisely, let A(x) :=
133: {n : n ≡ 1 or 3 (mod 6), n ≤ x} and S(x) := {n : there exists a 5-sparse STS(n) with n ≤ x}, then

136: linck [66] writes in his 2009 review of [70] “currently no nontrivial example of a 7-sparse Steiner triple
137: system is known”.

143: squares without the following subconfiguration?

158: 1.3   Sparse hypergraphs
159: 
160: Brown, Erdős and Sós [20, 12, 13] introduced the function fr (n, v, e) to denote the maximum number
161: of edges in an r-uniform hypergraph on n vertices which does not contain e edges spanned by v
162: vertices. Such hypergraphs are called G(v, e)-free (more precisely Gr (v, e)-free). They showed that
163: fr (n, e(r − k) + k, e) = Θ(nk ) for every 2 ≤ k < r and e ≥ 2, especially fr (n, e(r − 2) + 2, e) = Θ(n2 ).
164: The upper bound n2 / 2r is easy, and this was the source of Erdős’ conjecture concerning sparse
165:                          
166: 
167: Steiner systems. On the other hand, if we forbid e edges spanned by one more vertex this problems

160: Brown, Erdős and Sós [20, 12, 13] introduced the function fr (n, v, e) to denote the maximum number
161: of edges in an r-uniform hypergraph on n vertices which does not contain e edges spanned by v
162: vertices. Such hypergraphs are called G(v, e)-free (more precisely Gr (v, e)-free). They showed that
163: fr (n, e(r − k) + k, e) = Θ(nk ) for every 2 ≤ k < r and e ≥ 2, especially fr (n, e(r − 2) + 2, e) = Θ(n2 ).
164: The upper bound n2 / 2r is easy, and this was the source of Erdős’ conjecture concerning sparse
165:                          
166: 
167: Steiner systems. On the other hand, if we forbid e edges spanned by one more vertex this problems
168: becomes much more difficult. Brown, Erdős and Sós conjectured that
169: 
170:                                    fr (n, e(r − k) + k + 1, e) = o(nk ).                                (3)
171: 
172: One of the most famous results of this type is the (6, 3)-Theorem of Ruzsa and Szemerédi [61], the
173: case (e, k, r) = (3, 2, 3), saying that if no six points contain three triples then the size of the triple
174: system is o(n2 ), on the other hand n2−o(1) < f3 (n, 6, 3). This was extended by Erdős, Frankl, and
175: Rödl [25] for arbitrary fixed r ≥ 3,
176:

1:                                               Cross-intersecting sub-families of hereditary families
2:                                                                                       Peter Borg
3: 
4: 
5: 
6: 
7: arXiv:1103.3858v1 [math.CO] 20 Mar 2011
8:                                            Department of Mathematics, University of Malta, Msida MSD 2080, Malta
9:                                                                  p.borg.02@cantab.net
10:                                                                                 September 30, 2018
11: 
12: 
13:                                                                                         Abstract
14:                                                     Families A1 , A2 , ..., Ak of sets are said to be cross-intersecting if for any i and j
15:                                                 in {1, 2, ..., k} with i 6= j, any set in Ai intersects any set in Aj . For a finite set
16:                                                 X, let 2X denote the power set of X (the family of all subsets of X). A family H
17:                                                 is said to be hereditary if all subsets of any set in H are in H; so H is hereditary
18:                                                 if and only if it is a union of power sets. We conjecture that for any non-empty

14:                                                     Families A1 , A2 , ..., Ak of sets are said to be cross-intersecting if for any i and j
15:                                                 in {1, 2, ..., k} with i 6= j, any set in Ai intersects any set in Aj . For a finite set
16:                                                 X, let 2X denote the power set of X (the family of all subsets of X). A family H
17:                                                 is said to be hereditary if all subsets of any set in H are in H; so H is hereditary
18:                                                 if and only if it is a union of power sets. We conjecture that for any non-empty
19:                                                 hereditary sub-family H 6= {∅} of 2X and any k ≥ |X| + 1, both the sum and product
20:                                                 of sizes of k cross-intersecting sub-families A1 , A2 , ..., Ak (not necessarily distinct or
21:                                                 non-empty) of H are maxima if A1 = A2 = ... = Ak = S for some largest star S
22:                                                 of H (a sub-family of H whose sets have a common element). We prove this for the
23:                                                 case when H is compressed with respect to an element x of X, and for this purpose
24:                                                 we establish new properties of the usual compression operation. For the product, we
25:                                                 actually conjecture that the configuration A1 = A2 = ... = Ak = S is optimal for any
26:                                                 hereditary H and any k ≥ 2, and we prove this for a special case too.

29:                                           1    Basic definitions and notation
30:                                           Unless otherwise stated, we shall use small letters such as x to denote elements of a set or
31:                                           non-negative integers or functions, capital letters such as X to denote sets, and calligraphic
32:                                           letters such as F to denote families (i.e. sets whose elements are sets themselves). It is to
33:                                           be assumed that sets and families are finite. We call a set A an r-element set, or simply
34:                                           an r-set, if its size |A| is r (i.e. if it contains exactly r elements).
35:                                               For any integer n ≥ 1, the set {1, ..., n} of the ﬁrst n positive integers is denoted by [n].
36:                                           For a set X, the power set of X (i.e. {A : A ⊆ X}) is denoted by 2X , and the family of all
37:                                           r-element subsets of X is denoted by Xr .
38:                                               A family H is said to be a hereditary family (also called an ideal or a downset) if all

67: j ∈ F ∈ F and i ∈   / F.

70: 2    Intersecting sub-families of hereditary families
71: The following is a famous longstanding open conjecture in extremal set theory due to
72: Chvátal (see [7] for a more general conjecture).
73: 
74: Conjecture 2.1 ([10]) If H is a hereditary family, then H has the star property.
75: 
76: This conjecture was veriﬁed for the case when H is left-compressed by Chvátal [11] himself.
77: Snevily [17] took this result (together with results in [16, 18]) a signiﬁcant step forward by
78: verifying Conjecture 2.1 for the case when H is compressed with respect to an element x
79: of U(H).
80: 
81: Theorem 2.2 ([17]) If a hereditary family H is compressed with respect to an element x
82: of U(H), then H has the star property at x.
83: 
84: A generalisation is proved in [7] by means of an alternative self-contained argument.

277: 
278:                   7. Open problems and related questions
279:    The original motivation of this research was an analogue problem for partially
280: ordered sets (posets).
281:    A realizer is a set of linear extensions of the poset P , such that their intersection
282: (as relations) is P . The minimum cardinality of a realizer is the dimension of the
283: poset, a central notion in poset theory. The “standard example” Sn is the poset
284: formed by considering the 1-element subsets and the n − 1 element subsets of a set
285: of n elements, ordered by inclusion. It is well known that dim(Sn ) = n, but there
286: are posets of arbitrarily large dimensions without including even S3 as a subposet.
287:    Hiraguchi [5] proved that the dimension does not exceed half of the number of
288: elements of the poset. Bogart and Trotter [3] showed that for large n, the only
289: n-dimensional poset on 2n points is Sn . But what happens if the dimension is
290: slightly less than half the number of elements, is not known. We conjecture the
291: following.

286: are posets of arbitrarily large dimensions without including even S3 as a subposet.
287:    Hiraguchi [5] proved that the dimension does not exceed half of the number of
288: elements of the poset. Bogart and Trotter [3] showed that for large n, the only
289: n-dimensional poset on 2n points is Sn . But what happens if the dimension is
290: slightly less than half the number of elements, is not known. We conjecture the
291: following.
292: Conjecture 7.1. For every t < 1, but sufficiently close to 1 there is a c > 0, if a
293: poset has 2n points, and its dimension is at least tn, then it contains a standard
294: example of dimension cn.
295:    It is frequently noted that poset problems can be translated to graph theory
296: problems and vice versa by changing chromatic numbers of graphs to dimension
297: of posets, and cliques in graphs to standard examples in posets. Note that the
298: above conjecture would translate to the following statement: For every t < 1, but
299: sufficiently close to 1 there is a c > 0 such that if a graph has n points, and its
300: chromatic number is at least tn, then it contains a clique of cn points. This graph
301: version is trivial for all t > 1/2.
302:    The behavior of the sequence {Q(n, ⌈tn⌉)}∞     n=1 is also interesting in case t ≤
303: 1/2. On one hand,

1:                                                   BRIDGELAND STABILITY CONDITIONS ON THREEFOLDS I:
2:                                                        BOGOMOLOV-GIESEKER TYPE INEQUALITIES
3: 
4:                                                             AREND BAYER, EMANUELE MACRÌ, AND YUKINOBU TODA
5: 
6: 
7: 
8: 
9: arXiv:1103.5010v2 [math.AG] 27 Jun 2012
10:                                                   A BSTRACT. We construct new t-structures on the derived category of coherent sheaves on
11:                                                   smooth projective threefolds. We conjecture that they give Bridgeland stability conditions
12:                                                   near the large volume limit. We show that this conjecture is equivalent to a Bogomolov-
13:                                                   Gieseker type inequality for the third Chern character of certain stable complexes. We
14:                                                   also conjecture a stronger inequality, and prove it in the case of projective space, and for
15:                                                   various examples.
16:                                                      Finally, we prove a version of the classical Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality, not involv-
17:                                                   ing the third Chern character, for stable complexes.
18:

1:                                               Stasheff polytopes and the coordinate ring of the cluster
2:                                                                X -variety of type An
3:                                                                                     Linhui Shen

9:                                                                                       Abstract
10:                                                   We define Stasheff polytopes in the spaces of tropical points of cluster A-varieties.
11:                                                We study the supports of products of elements of canonical bases for cluster X -varieties.
12:                                                We prove that, for the cluster X -variety of type An , such supports are Stasheff poly-
13:                                                topes.
