35:                                           1    Introduction
36:                                           A cluster ensemble is a pair (X , A) of positive spaces defined in [FG]. Cluster A-varieties
37:                                           are closely related to cluster algebras introduced in [FZ] – the ring of regular functions on
38:                                           the space A coincides with the upper cluster algebra of [BFZ]. In this paper, we focus on
39:                                           the cluster X -variety XAn of type An , which is closely related to the moduli space M0,n+3 .
40: 
41:                                                                                            1
42: 
43:    In detail, let (AΦ , XΦ ) be the cluster ensemble assigned to a root system Φ of finite type.
44: Denote by AΦ∨ (Zt ) the space of Z-tropical points of the Langlands dual cluster A-variety
45: AΦ∨ . According to the Duality Conjectures from [FG], one should have an isomorphism

41:                                                                                            1
42: 
43:    In detail, let (AΦ , XΦ ) be the cluster ensemble assigned to a root system Φ of finite type.
44: Denote by AΦ∨ (Zt ) the space of Z-tropical points of the Langlands dual cluster A-variety
45: AΦ∨ . According to the Duality Conjectures from [FG], one should have an isomorphism
46:                                          IA :      AΦ∨ (Zt ) −→ E(XΦ )                          (1.1)
47: where E(XΦ ) is the set of indecomposable functions on XΦ , providing a canonical basis
48: of the ring of regular functions on XΦ . In the case of type An , such a map IA has been
49: constructed in [FG3].1 Denote by P(XAn ) the set of finite products of elements of E(XAn ).
50: The main goal of this paper is to study P(XAn ).
51:     The basis E(XAn ) has remarkable combinatorial properties. One of them is that, given
52: tropical points l1 , ..., lm ∈ AAn (Zt ), their corresponding product in P(XAn ) can be uniquely
53: decomposed into a finite sum of elements of E(XAn ) with non-negative coefficients:
54:                                 m
55:                                 Y                    X
56:                                       IA (li ) =                   c(l1 , ..., lm ; l)IA (l).   (1.2)
57:                                 i=1                l∈AAn   (Zt )

56:                                       IA (li ) =                   c(l1 , ..., lm ; l)IA (l).   (1.2)
57:                                 i=1                l∈AAn   (Zt )
58: 
59: The support of the product is the set of l ∈ AAn (Zt ) such that c(l1 , ..., lm ; l) 6= 0. The
60: Convexity Conjecture from [FG] suggests that the support is universally convex in AAn (Zt ).
61: We prove that it is not only convex but also a Stasheff polytope.
62:     The Stasheff polytope is a remarkable convex polytope first described combinatorially
63: by J. Stasheff in 1963. In Section 1.3, we define Stasheff polytopes in tropical positive spaces.
64: The main idea of the definition is given below.
65:     Denote by A(Rt ) the space of R-tropical points of a positive space A. It has a piecewise-
66: linear structure, isomorphic to Rdim A in many different ways. We define a convex polytope
67: in A(Rt ) as the intersection of “half-spaces” given by inequalities of tropical indecompos-
68: able functions (see Section 1.1). The tropical indecomposable functions are usually not
69: linear in all coordinate systems, but are convex and piecewise linear.
70:     Let Φ≥−1 be the set of positive and simple negative roots of a root system Φ of finite
71: type. In [CFZ], polytopal realizations of Stasheff polytopes (or generalized associahedra)
72: in Rn were constructed by a set of linear inequalities indexed by Φ≥−1 . For the cluster A-
73: variety AΦ , there are cluster variables Aα indexed by Φ≥−1 ([FZ1, Theorem 5.7]). When

88:                                                              2
89: 
90: It is a Stasheff polytope in the space AAn (Zt ).
91: 
92:     We conjecture the same result for any root system Φ of finite type.
93:     In Rn the Minkowski sum of finitely many points is the sum of these points as vectors.
94: It is still a point.
95:     In our case, AAn has many coordinate systems which are called clusters. Each cluster
96: provides a way of taking the sum of the tropical points as vectors. We thus get a set of
97: vertices parametrized by the set of clusters. The Minkowski sum turns out to be the convex
98: hull of these vertices. It is a Stasheff polytope.
99:     An example of Stasheff polytopes in AA2 is shown on Fig. 1-5. These figures show the
100: same polytope in 5 different coordinate systems (clusters). Notice that it is a “tropical”
101: pentagon, but looks like a heptagon from the usual point of view: the reason is that some
102: of its sides are given by piecewise linear functions, and thus they are not line segments.
103: But they become line segments in other coordinate systems. In general, the Minkowski
104: sum gives rise to a new family of convex polytopes in tropical positive spaces (Definition
105: 3.15).

114: XAn . The induced map k ∗ on their coordinate rings takes E(XAn ) (respectively P(XAn ))
115: into E(AeAn ) (respectively P(AeAn )). By using Theorem 2.10 and the map k, we prove
116: the first part of Theorem 1.1. The second part is a direct consequence of Theorem 1.10.
117: Theorem 3.14 provides a bijection between P∗ (XAn ) and the set of Stasheff polytopes in
118: AAn (Zt ). Conjecture 3.16 is a generalization of Theorem 1.10 to all cluster X -varieties.

13:                                                   δ(G) ≥ 23 n contains the square of a hamiltonian cycle. In 1996 Fan and Kierstead
14:                                                   proved the path version of Pósa’s Conjecture. They also proved that it would suffice
15:                                                   to show that G contains the square of a cycle of length greater than 23 n. Still in 1996,
16:                                                   Komlós, Sárközy, and Szemerédi proved Pósa’s Conjecture, using the Regularity and
17:                                                   Blow-up Lemmas, for graphs of order n ≥ n0 , where n0 is a very large constant. Here
18:                                                   we show without using these lemmas that n0 := 2 × 108 is sufficient. We are motivated

11:                                                                                          Abstract
12:                                                       In 1962 Pósa conjectured that every graph G on n vertices with minimum degree
13:                                                   δ(G) ≥ 23 n contains the square of a hamiltonian cycle. In 1996 Fan and Kierstead
14:                                                   proved the path version of Pósa’s Conjecture. They also proved that it would suffice
15:                                                   to show that G contains the square of a cycle of length greater than 23 n. Still in 1996,
16:                                                   Komlós, Sárközy, and Szemerédi proved Pósa’s Conjecture, using the Regularity and
17:                                                   Blow-up Lemmas, for graphs of order n ≥ n0 , where n0 is a very large constant. Here
18:                                                   we show without using these lemmas that n0 := 2 × 108 is sufficient. We are motivated
19:                                                   by the recent work of Levitt, Szemerédi and Sárközy, but our methods are based on
20:                                                   techniques that were available in the 90’s.

1:                                           On a Conjecture of Brouwer Involving the Connectivity
2:                                                       of Strongly Regular Graphs
3:                                                       Sebastian M. Cioabă∗, Kijung Kim† and Jack H. Koolen‡

14:                                                                                        Abstract

11: 
12:                                                              Dedicated to the 60th birthday of Andries E. Brouwer
13: 
14:                                                                                        Abstract
15:                                                    In this paper, we study a conjecture of Andries E. Brouwer from 1996 regarding
16:                                                the minimum number of vertices of a strongly regular graph whose removal disconnects
17:                                                the graph into non-singleton components.
18:                                                    We show that strongly regular graphs constructed from copolar spaces and from
19:                                                the more general spaces called ∆-spaces are counterexamples to Brouwer’s Conjec-
20:                                                ture. Using J.I. Hall’s characterization of finite reduced copolar spaces, we find that
21:                                                the triangular graphs T (m), the symplectic graphs Sp(2r, q) over the field Fq (for
22:                                                any q prime power), and the strongly regular graphs constructed from the hyperbolic
23:                                                quadrics O + (2r, 2) and from the elliptic quadrics O − (2r, 2) over the field F2 , respec-
24:                                                tively, are counterexamples to Brouwer’s Conjecture. For each of these graphs, we
25:                                                determine precisely the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects the
26:                                                graph into non-singleton components. While we are not aware of an analogue of Hall’s
27:                                                characterization theorem for ∆-spaces, we show that complements of the point graphs
28:                                                of certain finite generalized quadrangles are point graphs of ∆-spaces and thus, yield

20:                                                ture. Using J.I. Hall’s characterization of finite reduced copolar spaces, we find that
21:                                                the triangular graphs T (m), the symplectic graphs Sp(2r, q) over the field Fq (for
22:                                                any q prime power), and the strongly regular graphs constructed from the hyperbolic
23:                                                quadrics O + (2r, 2) and from the elliptic quadrics O − (2r, 2) over the field F2 , respec-
24:                                                tively, are counterexamples to Brouwer’s Conjecture. For each of these graphs, we
25:                                                determine precisely the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects the
26:                                                graph into non-singleton components. While we are not aware of an analogue of Hall’s
27:                                                characterization theorem for ∆-spaces, we show that complements of the point graphs
28:                                                of certain finite generalized quadrangles are point graphs of ∆-spaces and thus, yield
29:                                                other counterexamples to Brouwer’s Conjecture.
30:                                                    We prove that Brouwer’s Conjecture is true for many families of strongly regular
31:                                                graphs including the conference graphs, the generalized quadrangles GQ(q, q) graphs,
32:                                                the lattice graphs, the Latin square graphs, the strongly regular graphs with smallest

=== 1105.1764 ===

1:                                                                             Generating p-extremal graphs
2:                                                                                         Derrick Stolee∗
3:                                                                                  Department of Mathematics
4:                                                                                Department of Computer Science

17:                                                                                               Abstract
18:                                                        Define f (n, p) to be the maximum number of edges in a graph on n vertices with p perfect

22:                                                    on Lovász’s Two Ear Theorem and structural results of Hartke, Stolee, West, and Yancey, we
23:                                                    develop a computational method for determining cp and generating the finite set of graphs which
24:                                                    describe the infinite family of p-extremal graphs. This method extends the knowledge of the
25:                                                    size and structure of p-extremal graphs from p ≤ 10 to p ≤ 27. These values provide further
26:                                                    evidence towards a conjectured upper bound and prove the sequence cp is not monotonic.
27: 
28: 
29:                                          1        Introduction
30:                                          A perfect matching is a set of disjoint edges which cover all vertices. Let Φ(G) be the number of
31:                                          perfect matchings in a graph G. For even n and positive p, the function f (n, p) is the maximum
32:                                          number of edges in a graph G on n vertices with Φ(G) = p. The exact behavior of f (n, p) is not
33:                                          completely understood. This work extends the current knowledge on this problem by applying
34:                                          Lovász’a Two Ear Theorem [4] and structure theorems of Hartke, Stolee, West, and Yancey [3]
35:                                          with an isomorph-free generation scheme [9] in order to compute f (n, p) for all p ≤ 27 as well as
36:                                          determine the exact structure of graphs meeting these extremal values.
37:                                                  Hetyei first characterized the extremal graphs with a single perfect matching and n vertices
38:                                                                                                              2
39:                                          (unpublished; see [5, Corollary 5.3.14]) giving f (n, 1) = n4 for all even n. Dudek and Schmitt [1]

87: executing this search, we compute the sequence cp and describe the structure of p-extremal graphs
88: for all p ≤ 27. We begin by discussing the structure of p-extremal graphs in Section 2. Sections
89: 3 through 7 contain the description of the computational technique, which are described in more

12:                                                                               Abstract
13:                                                    Let F be a finite union-closed family of sets whose largest set con-
14:                                                tains n elements. In [6], Wójcik defined the density of F to be the
15:                                                ratio of the average set size of F to n and conjectured that the mini-
16:                                                mum density over all union-closed families whose largest set contains n
17:                                                elements is (1 + o(1)) log2 n/(2n) as n → ∞. We use a result of Reimer
18:                                                [3] to show that the density of F is always at least log2 n/(2n), verify-
19:                                                ing Wójcik’s conjecture. As a corollary
20:                                                                                  p      we show that for n ≥ 16, some
21:                                                element must appear in at least (log2 n)/n(|F |/2) sets of F .

1:                                              On positivity of principal minors of bivariate Bézier
2:                                                               collocation matrix
3: 
4: 
5: 
6: 
7: arXiv:1106.0631v2 [math.NA] 14 Aug 2015
8:                                                                       Gašper Jakliča , Tadej Kanduč∗,b
9:                                                 a
10:                                                     FMF and IMFM, University of Ljubljana and PINT, University of Primorska,

18:                                           Abstract

18:                                           Abstract
19:                                           It is well known that the bivariate polynomial interpolation problem at uni-
20:                                           formly distributed domain points of a triangle is correct. Thus the corre-
21:                                           sponding interpolation matrix M is nonsingular. L. L. Schumaker stated the
22:                                           conjecture that all principal submatrices of M are nonsingular too. Fur-
23:                                           thermore, all of the corresponding determinants (the principal minors) are
24:                                           conjectured to be positive. This result would solve the constrained interpola-
25:                                           tion problem. In this paper, the conjecture on minors for polynomial degree
26:                                           ≤ 17 and conjecture for some particular configurations of domain points are
27:                                           confirmed.
28:                                           Key words: collocation matrix, principal minor, bivariate Bernstein
29:                                           polynomial, positivity.
30:                                           2000 MSC: 65D05, 65F40.

26:                                           ≤ 17 and conjecture for some particular configurations of domain points are

69: The authors of [8] verified the theorem by computer only. In [5], a proof of
70: nonsingularity of principal matrices of the collocation matrix for some special
71: configurations of domain points is provided.
72:      A straightforward way of verifying the conjecture by computing principal
73: minors of M is time consuming due to the exponential growth of the number
74: of subsets that need to be analysed, and cannot be done within a reasonable
75: time for d > 7 using current computational facilities. For example, for d = 10
76:                                   10+2

78:      Matrices with positive principal minors are called P-matrices. A lot of
79: their properties are known, see [9, 10], e.g. Unfortunately, this theory could
80: not be applied for the study of the problem at hand, so a different approach,
81: based on positive definiteness, is used.
82:      In this paper, the conjecture on positivity of principal minors of the bi-
83: variate Bézier collocation matrix M is confirmed for arbitrary Γ ⊂ Id for
84: d ≤ 17. Thus the corresponding constrained Lagrange interpolation problem

78:      Matrices with positive principal minors are called P-matrices. A lot of
79: their properties are known, see [9, 10], e.g. Unfortunately, this theory could
80: not be applied for the study of the problem at hand, so a different approach,
81: based on positive definiteness, is used.
82:      In this paper, the conjecture on positivity of principal minors of the bi-
83: variate Bézier collocation matrix M is confirmed for arbitrary Γ ⊂ Id for
84: d ≤ 17. Thus the corresponding constrained Lagrange interpolation problem
85: has a unique solution. This covers all the cases useful in practice, since it
86: is well known that polynomials of high degrees have undesired properties.
87: Some particular configurations of domain points are analysed. A conjecture
88: for exact lower bound of det MΓ is stated. The paper is concluded by some
89: remarks and comments on future work.

1:                                                          WEIGHTED PEBBLING NUMBERS OF GRAPHS
2: 
3:                                             STEPHANIE JONES, JOSHUA D. LAISON, CAMERON MCLEMAN, AND KATHRYN NYMAN
4: 
5:                                                 Abstract. We expand the theory of pebbling to graphs with weighted edges. In a weighted
6:                                                 pebbling game, one player distributes a set amount of weight on the edges of a graph and
7:                                                 his opponent chooses a target vertex and places a configuration of pebbles on the vertices.
8:                                                 Player one wins if, through a series of pebbling moves, he can move at least one pebble to
9: 
10: 
11: 
12: 
13: arXiv:1106.1625v1 [math.CO] 8 Jun 2011
14:                                                 the target. A pebbling move of p pebbles across an edge with weight w leaves bpwc pebbles
15:                                                 on the next vertex. We find the weighted pebbling numbers of stars, graphs with at least
16:                                                 2|V | − 1 edges, and trees with given targets. We give an explicit formula for the minimum
17:                                                 total weight required on the edges of a length-2 path, solvable with p pebbles and exhibit
18:                                                 a graph which requires an edge with weight 1/3 in order to achieve its weighted pebbling

24:                                                                                   1. Introduction
25:                                             Graph pebbling originated two decades ago as a technique to prove a result on zero-sum
26:                                          sequences in finite groups [3]. Since then the subject has come into its own as an active
27:                                          area of research in graph theory, with over 50 papers by 80 authors [10, 9]. Problems in the
28:                                          field, guiding current research, include Graham’s Conjecture on pebbling numbers of graph
29:                                          products [6, 7, 8, 11], and pebbling numbers of graphs with small diameter [1, 2, 4].
30:                                             One interpretation of the pebbling number arises from the pebbling game we play against
31:                                          an evil opponent. In this game, the evil opponent distributes some number of pebbles on
32:                                          the vertices of a graph G and chooses a target vertex. Then we make pebbling moves on
33:                                          G. A pebbling move across an edge decreases the number of pebbles by a factor of 1/2,
34:                                          rounded down (see Figure 1). If we can reach the target with one or more pebbles, we win
35:                                          the game. The pebbling number of G is the smallest number of pebbles that we can give the
36:                                          evil opponent and always win.
37:                                             We can imagine a pebbling move across an edge uv as multiplying the number of pebbles
38:                                          being moved from u to v by 1/2. In this way, 1/2 can be thought of as the weight or cost
39:                                          on edge uv. We generalize this idea to weighted graphs so that a pebbling move from u to
40:                                          v multiplies the number of pebbles being moved by the weight on edge uv, rounded down.
41:                                          As before, we can reinterpret these definitions game-theoretically. Namely, we introduce the

764: Conjecture 15. All trees require the full weight |E(G)|/2 to achieve their weighted pebbling
765: number. That is, for a tree T , wT (wp(T )) = |E(G)|/2.

17:                                           characteristics of a family of linear operators, the joint spectral radius (in short
18:                                           JSR) and the lower spectral radius (in short LSR), which are well-known different

53: 2                                                      Nicola Guglielmi, Vladimir Protasov

56: the efficiency of the new method we are able to apply it in order to give answers
57: to several conjectures which have been recently stated in combinatorics, number
58: theory, and the theory of formal languages.
59: Keywords Linear operator · joint spectral radius · lower spectral radius ·

64: 1 Introduction and background
65: 
66: The joint spectral characteristics of linear operators are now applied in many
67: areas, from functional analysis and dynamical systems to discrete mathematics
68: and number theory. We focus on two characteristics: the joint spectral radius and
69: the lower spectral radius, and elaborate a method of their exact computation
70: applicable even for relatively high dimensions.

176: families. The results of many numerical tests with randomly generated matrices
177: of dimension from 5 to 100 (some of them are presented at the end of this paper)
178: confirm this claim. In all the cases the algorithms found the exact values of JSR.
179: As a further confirmation of this, we are able to apply the new method to solve
180: several open problems in combinatorics and discrete mathematics.
181:      In the literature there are several methods for the computation of the JSR.
182: Some of them work only for small dimensions d, but give either an exact or a very
183: accurate value of ρb. For example, the method of polytope norms [P1, GZ1, BJP,
184: GZ2, CGSZ]; see also special methods in [V, P3, HMR] elaborated for particular
185: matrices.
186:      Other methods aim to an approximate computation, such as the Kronecker
187: lifting method [P2, BN], ellipsoidal norm method [BNT], Gripenberg’s branch-
188: and-bound method [DL, G] can work for bigger dimensions (mostly, up to 20),
189: but produce pretty rough estimations. Recent approaches involving some modern
190: tools of convex optimization (conic and semidefinite programming, sum-of-squares
191: approximation, etc.) have rather good accuracy for higher dimensions (10 or even
192: bigger) [PJ, PJB]. Most of those methods are actually based on the same simple
193: idea. For each k we have

413:      In Section 8 we consider applications to several problems of combinatorics,
414: coding theory and number theory. In the problem of asymptotic growth of the
415: number of overlap-free words (§8.1) we compute precise values of exponents of the
416: upper and lower growth. This proves two conjectures stated in 2008 [JPB2]. Then
417: in §8.2 we do the same for the problem of density of ones in the Pascal rhombus,
418: and disprove one previously know conjecture. In §8.3 and §8.4 we find precise
419: values of the lower and upper growths of the Euler partition functions for some
420: values of the parameters.
421:      Section 9 presents the results of numerical tests for JSR and LSR computation
422: for randomly generated matrices of dimensions from 5 to 100. In all the cases
423: the algorithms find the exact values of JSR and LSR, which suggests that our
424: approach generically has finite convergence.

1:                                           Knots in the Canonical Book Representation of Complete
2: 
3: 
4: 
5: 
6: arXiv:1106.4065v1 [math.GT] 21 Jun 2011
7:                                                                   Graphs
8:                                                                    Andrea Politano and Dana Rowland
9:                                                                                   June 16, 2011
10: 
11: 
12:                                                                                       Abstract
13:                                                We examine K  e n , the canonical book representation of the complete graph Kn , and describe
14:                                            knots that can be obtained as cycles in this particular spatial embedding.
15:                                                                                                                          We prove that for
16:                                            each knotted Hamiltonian cycle α in K     e n , there are at least 2k n+k Hamiltonian cycles that
17:                                                                                                                   k
18:                                            are ambient isotopic to α in K    e n+k . We show that when p and q are relatively prime with

58: at least 7 non-trivially knotted 7-cycles, a canonical book representation is minimally linked
59: and knotted in this sense.
60:     In addition, Fleming and Mellor proved that a canonical book representation of Kn attains
61: 14 n7 triangle-square links, and showed this is the minimum possible for any embedding of
62: Kn [6]. They also conjectured that for any graph G there is some book representation that
63: realizes the minimal number of non-trivial links possible in an embedding of G.
64:     Similarly, the canonical book representation K  e n is a candidate for the minimal number of
65: knotted cycles in an embedding.
66:     In this paper, we focus on which knots arise as knotted Hamiltonian cycles in the canonical
67: book representation for n > 7. In section 2 we review the definitions of book representations
68: and Otsuki’s canonical book representation, and show how knotted cycles in K       e n are related to
69:                     e                                e
70: knotted cycles in Kn+1 . In section 3 we show that Kn contains a (p, q) torus knot (or link) when
71: n ≥ q + 2p. In section 4 we examine composite knots in the canonical book representation,
72: and in section 5 we give a listing of all the knots that appear as cycles in K   e n for 8 ≤ n ≤ 11
73:                                                  e
74: and we conjecture about the ways in which Kn may achieve the minimal possible knotting
75: complexity.

78: 2      The Canonical Book Representation of Kn
79: In this section, we review the right canonical book representation, as defined in [9]. (In the
80: right canonical book representation, the knotted 7-cycles are right-handed trefoil knots. The
81: left canonical book representation is the mirror image of the one presented here.)
82: 
83: Definition 1. A k-book is a subset of R3 consisting of a line L and distinct half-planes
84: S1 ,S2 ,...,Sk with boundary L. The line L forms the spine of the book and the half-planes Si form
85: the pages, or sheets. We denote a k-book by Bk . Let G be a graph, and let f : G → Bk ⊂ R3 be
86: 
87: 

738: 
739:     In [7], Hirano proves that all spatial embeddings of K8 must have at least 3 knotted
740: Hamiltonian cycles; however, no known example achieves that bound. In Proposition 18 of
741: [1], Abrams and Mellor proved that the minimum number of knotted cycles in K8 must be
742: between 15 and 29. We conjecture the following:
743: Conjecture 1. The canonical book representation of Kn contains the fewest total number of
744: knotted cycles possible in any embedding of Kn .
745: Conjecture 2. The canonical book representation of Kn contains the fewest number of knotted
746: Hamiltonian cycles possible in any embedding of Kn .
747:    Note that Conjecture 2 implies Conjecture 1. Both conjectures are true for n ≤ 7.

180: William Hamilton:
181:      “If a figure be anyhow divided. . . four colours may be wanted but
182:      not more”
183: However from this point on, neither of the Guthrie brothers, de Morgan nor
184: Hamilton had anything to do with the problem! The conjecture was first pub-
185: lished by Cayley in his paper of 1879, although he did attribute the conjecture
186: to de Morgan.
187:      The first “proof” was also published in 1879 by Alfred Kempe and it met
188: with wide acclaim and it wasn’t until 11 years later in 1890 that Heawood
189: highlighted fatal flaws in Kempe’s proof.
190:      In his paper of 1890 [1], Heawood found faults in Kempe’s proof but was un-
191: able to fix them and so instead he tried to explore other areas of map colouring;
192: it is these ideas that Heawood thought of back then that will form the basis for
193: our discussions in this report. It was a prolific paper in which Heawood posed
194: several extensions including:
195:    • What if we change the surface? (i.e. a doughnut instead of a sphere)
196:    • What if we allow “empires” or colonies? (such as USA and Alaska)
197:    • What if we allow colonies on another body such as the moon?

1:                                           Intersecting families of sets and permutations:
2:                                                               a survey
3: 
4: 
5: 
6: 
7: arXiv:1106.6144v1 [math.CO] 30 Jun 2011
8:                                                                              Peter Borg
9: 
10:                                                    Department of Mathematics, University of Malta,
11:                                                               Msida MSD 2080, Malta
12:                                                               p.borg.02@cantab.net
13:                                                                          October 29, 2018
14: 
15:                                                                                 Abstract
16:                                                     A family A of sets is said to be t-intersecting if any two sets in A
17:                                                 have at least t common elements. A central problem in extremal set
18:                                                 theory is to determine the size or structure of a largest t-intersecting

16:                                                     A family A of sets is said to be t-intersecting if any two sets in A
17:                                                 have at least t common elements. A central problem in extremal set
18:                                                 theory is to determine the size or structure of a largest t-intersecting
19:                                                 sub-family of a given family F. We give a survey of known results, con-
20:                                                 jectures and open problems for various important families F, namely,
21:                                                 power sets, levels of power sets, hereditary families, families of signed
22:                                                 sets, families of labeled sets, and families of permutations. We also
23:                                                 provide some extensions and consequences of known results.
24: 
25: 
26:                                           1    Introduction
27:                                           Unless otherwise stated, we shall use small letters such as x to denote ele-
28:                                           ments of a set or non-negative integers or functions, capital letters such as X
29:                                           to denote sets, and calligraphic letters such as F to denote families (i.e. sets
30:                                           whose elements are sets themselves). It is to be assumed that arbitrary sets
31:                                           and families are finite. We call a set A an r-element set, or simply an r-set,
32:                                           if its size |A| is r (i.e. if it contains exactly r elements). A family is said to
33:                                           be uniform if all its sets are of the same size.

69: Problem: Given a family F and an integer t ≥ 1, determine the size or
70: structure of a largest t-intersecting sub-family of F .
71: 
72: We provide a survey of results that answer this question for families that
73: are of particular importance, and we also point out open problems and con-
74: jectures. The survey papers [25] and [32] cover a few of the results we mention
75: here and also go into many variations of the above problem, however, much
76: progress has been made since their publication. Here we cover many of the
77: important results that have been established to date, restricting ourselves to
78: the problem stated above.
79:     The most obvious families to consider are the power set 2[n] and the uni-
80: form sub-family [n] r
81:                       , and in fact the problem for these families has been
82: solved completely. However, there are other important families on which
83: much progress has been made, and there are others that are still subject to
84: much investigation. The families deﬁned below are perhaps the ones that
85: have received most attention and that we will be concerned with.
86:

334: 3    Intersecting sub-families of hereditary fami-
335:      lies
336: Recall that 2[n] has the star property. Also recall that the power set of a
337: set X is the simplest example of a hereditary family as 2X is a hereditary
338: family with only one base (X). An outstanding open problem in extremal
339: set theory is the following conjecture (see [14] for a more general conjecture).
340: 
341: Conjecture 3.1 ([19]) If H is a hereditary family, then H has the star
342: property.
343: 
344: Chvátal [20] veriﬁed this conjecture for the case when H is left-compressed
345: (i.e. H ⊆ 2[n] and (H\{j}) ∪ {i} ∈ H whenever 1 ≤ i < j ∈ H ∈ H and
346: i ∈/ H). Snevily [54] took this result (together with results in [53, 55]) a
347: signiﬁcant step forward by verifying Conjecture 3.1 for the case when H is
348: compressed with respect to an element x of U(H) (i.e. (H\{h}) ∪ {x} ∈ H
349: whenever h ∈ H ∈ H and x ∈  / H).
350: 
351: Theorem 3.2 ([54]) If a hereditary family H is compressed with respect to

349: whenever h ∈ H ∈ H and x ∈  / H).

352: an element x of U(H), then H has the star property at {x}.

1:                                                                             Identifying codes in line graphs✩
2: 
3:                                                      Florent Foucauda , Sylvain Gravierb, Reza Naserasra,b, Aline Parreaub, Petru Valicova
4:                                                      a LaBRI - Université de Bordeaux - CNRS, 351 cours de la Libération, 33405 Talence cedex, France.
5:                                                             b Institut Fourier 100, rue des Maths, BP 74, 38402 St Martin d’Hères cedex, France.
6: 
7: 
8: 
9: 
10:                                           Abstract
11: 
12: 
13: 
14: 
15: arXiv:1107.0207v2 [math.CO] 21 Sep 2012
16:                                           An identifying code of a graph is a subset of its vertices such that every vertex of the graph is uniquely
17:                                           identified by the set of its neighbours within the code. We study the edge-identifying code problem, i.e.
18:                                           the identifying code problem in line graphs. If γ ID (G) denotes the size of a minimum identifying code of an

21: 
22: 
23:                                           can be improved to Θ( n) in the class of line graphs. Moreover, this bound is tight. We also prove that the
24:                                           upper bound γ ID (L(G)) ≤ 2|V (G)| − 5, where L(G) is the line graph of G, holds (with two exceptions). This
25:                                           implies that a conjecture of R. Klasing, A. Kosowski, A. Raspaud and the first author holds for a subclass
26:                                           of line graphs. Finally, we show that the edge-identifying code problem is NP-complete, even for the class
27:                                           of planar bipartite graphs of maximum degree 3 and arbitrarily large girth.
28:                                           Keywords: Identifying codes, Dominating sets, Line graphs, NP-completeness.
29: 
30: 
31: 
32:                                           1. Introduction
33: 
34:                                              An identifying code of a graph G is a subset C of vertices of G such that for each vertex x, the set of
35:                                           vertices in C at distance at most 1 from x, is nonempty and uniquely identifies x. More formally:
36:                                           Definition 1. Given a graph G, a subset C of V (G) is an identifying code of G if C is both:
37:                                              • a dominating set of G, i.e. for each vertex v ∈ V (G), N [v] ∩ C 6= ∅, and
38:                                              • a separating set of G, i.e. for each pair u, v ∈ V (G) (u 6= v), N [u] ∩ C 6= N [v] ∩ C.

47:                                           bounds in terms of simpler graph parameters. Examples of classic results are as follows:
48:                                           Theorem 2. [12] If G is a twin-free graph with at least two edges, then γ ID (G) ≤ |V (G)| − 1.
49:                                               The collection of all twin-free graphs reaching this bound is classified in [8].
50:                                               A better upper bound in terms of both number of vertices and maximum degree ∆(G) of a graph G is
51:                                           also conjectured:

66:   Some support for this conjecture is provided in [8, 9, 10].
67:   The parameter γ ID (G) is also bounded below by a function of |V (G)| where equality holds for infinitely
68: many graphs.
69: Theorem 4. [13] For any twin-free graph G, γ ID (G) ≥ ⌈log2 (|V (G)| + 1)⌉.
70:      The collection of all graphs attaining this lower bound is classified in [16].
71:      From a computational point of view, it is shown that given a graph G, finding the exact value of γ ID (G)
72: is in the class of NP-hard problems. It in fact remains NP-hard for many subclasses of graphs [1, 4].
73: Furthermore, approximating γ ID (G) is not easy either as shown in [14, 11, 17]: it is NP-hard to approximate
74: γ ID (G) within a o(log(|V (G)|))-factor.
75:      The problem of finding identifying codes in graphs can be viewed as a special case of the more general
76: combinatorial problem of finding transversals in hypergraphs (a transversal is a set of vertices intersecting
77: each hyperedge). More precisely, to each graph G one can associate the hypergraph H(G) whose vertices are
78: vertices of G and whose hyperedges are all the sets of the form N [v] and N [u] ⊖ N [v] (symmetric difference
79: of N [u] and N [v]). Finding an identifying code for G is then equivalent to finding a transversal for H(G).

217: G2 . If G1 and G2 are both of order 1, then both of their vertices must be in the same part of the graph as
218: otherwise the edge connecting them is not dominated by CE . But now for any vertex x which is not in the
219: same part as G1 and G2 , the edges connecting x to G1 and G2 are not separated.

221: line graphs. These examples were first introduced in [9] but without using the notion of edge-identifying
222: codes.

553:    We recall that a graph on n vertices is 2-degenerated if its vertices can be ordered v1 , v2 , . . . , vn such
554: that each vertex vi is joined to at most two vertices in {v1 , v2 , . . . , vi−1 }. Our main idea for proving upper
555: bounds is to show that given a pendant-free graph G, any (inclusionwise) minimal edge-identifying code CE
556: induces a 2-degenerated subgraph of G and hence |CE | ≤ 2|V (G)| − 3. Our proofs are constructive and one
557: could build such small edge-identifying codes.

87:     The dismal primes are the subject of §3. A necessary condition for a number to be a
88: prime is that it contain a digit equal to b − 1. The data suggest that if k is large, almost all
89: numbers of length k containing b − 1 as a digit and not ending with zero are prime, and so
90: the number of primes of length k appears to approach (b−1)2 bk−2 as k → ∞ (Conjecture 1).
91: In any case, any number with a digit equal to b − 1 is a product of primes (Theorem 7), and
92: every number can be written as r times a product of primes, for some r ∈ {0, 1, . . . , b − 1}
93:    1

95: of squares, so there is no dismal analogue of the four-squares theorem.

127: to note that one of the first problems in which the asymptotic behavior was shown to involve

135:     The sum-of-divisors function σb (n) is the subject of §7. There are analogues of the
136: perfect numbers, although they seem not to be as interesting as in the classical case. Section

138: formulas for the number of partitions of n into distinct parts.
139:     This is the second of a series of articles dealing with various kinds of carryless arithmetic,
140: and contains a report of our investigations into dismal arithmetic carried out during the

409:     We can get a lower bound on πb (k) by producing large numbers of primes, using the
410: process of “promotion.” We call a base b number with at least two digits a pseudoprime if
411: its only factorizations are of the form n = p b q where at least one of p and q has length
412: 1. In base b, n is a prime if and only if it is a pseudoprime and contains a digit β. If
413: nk−1 nk−2 ...n0 |b is a pseudoprime and r is its maximal digit, then nk−1 nk−2 ...n0 |r+1 is a base
414: r + 1 prime and furthermore nk−1 nk−2 ...n0 |c is a pseudoprime in any base c ≥ r + 1. In base
415: 2 there is no difference between primes and pseudoprimes. As long as we exclude numbers
416: ending with 0, reversing the digits of a number does not change its status as a prime or
417: pseudoprime.
418:     The advantage of working with pseudoprimes rather than primes is that the inverse image
419: of a pseudoprime under a digit map (see §2) is again a pseudoprime.

1:                                                   Large matchings in uniform hypergraphs and the conjectures of
2:                                                                        Erdős and Samuels
3:

7: arXiv:1107.1219v2 [math.CO] 31 Jan 2012

9:                                                                                         Benny Sudakovk

13:                                                                                              Abstract
14: 

13:                                                                                              Abstract
14: 
15:                                                       In this paper we study degree conditions which guarantee the existence of perfect match-
16:                                                    ings and perfect fractional matchings in uniform hypergraphs. We reduce this problem to an
17:                                                    old conjecture by Erdős on estimating the maximum number of edges in a hypergraph when
18:                                                    the (fractional) matching number is given, which we are able to solve in some special cases us-
19:                                                    ing probabilistic techniques. Based on these results, we obtain some general theorems on the
20:                                                    minimum d-degree ensuring the existence of perfect (fractional) matchings. In particular, we
21:                                                    asymptotically determine the minimum vertex degree which guarantees a perfect matching in
22:                                                    4-uniform and 5-uniform hypergraphs. We also discuss an application to a problem of ﬁnding an
23:                                                    optimal data allocation in a distributed storage system.

7:                                                    Abstract. We study graphs whose chromatic number is close to the order of the graph (the
8: 
9: 
10: 
11: 
12: arXiv:1107.2630v1 [math.CO] 13 Jul 2011
13:                                                    number of vertices). Both when the chromatic number is a constant multiple of the order and
14:                                                    when the difference of the chromatic number and the order is a small fixed number, large cliques
15:                                                    are forced. We study the latter situation, and we give quantitative results how large the clique
16:                                                    number of these graphs have to be. Some related questions are discussed and conjectures are
17:                                                    posed.
18:                                                       Please note that the results of this article were significantly generalized. Therefore this paper

12: arXiv:1107.2630v1 [math.CO] 13 Jul 2011
13:                                                    number of vertices). Both when the chromatic number is a constant multiple of the order and

16:                                                    number of these graphs have to be. Some related questions are discussed and conjectures are
17:                                                    posed.
18:                                                       Please note that the results of this article were significantly generalized. Therefore this paper
19:                                                    will never be published in a journal. See instead [1] for the more general results.

24:                                                                                          1. Introduction
25:                                              It is well known that for all positive integer c, triangle free graphs with chromatic number c exist.
26:                                           The number of vertices required to obtain such graphs grows quadratically with c. So when the
27:                                           chromatic number is close to the order of the graph, large cliques are forced. But just how large
28:                                           these cliques have to be? To make these questions precise, we introduce the following definition.
29:                                                                        Q(n, c) = min{ω(G) : |V (G)| = n and χ(G) = c}

57: set, then there is a Kn−2k+2 and we are done. If S is an independent set, then remove S from G.
58: The remaining graph G′ has |V (G′ )| = n − k and χ(G′ ) ≥ n − k − 1, so there is a clique of size
59: n − k − 1 ≥ n − 2k + 2 in G′ and in turn in G.                                                 
60: Corollary 1.2. Q(n, n − 3) = n − 4
61:     Based on these, the author (naively) made the following conjecture.
62: Conjecture 1.3. Let k be a positive integer. If n is large enough, then
63:                                     Q(n, n − k) = n − 2k + ⌈k/2⌉.
64:    This conjecture was disproven in [1].
65:    Jahanbekam and West [6] observed that Q(n, n − k) is at most the conjectured value whenever
66: n ≥ ⌈5k/2⌉ and they conjecture that this threshold on n is both sufficient and necessary for equality.
67: Their constructions are the following. If k is even, let r = k/2 and let G be the complement of rC5 .
68: If k is odd, let r = (k + 1)/2 and let G be complement of (r − 1)C5 + P3 . Then adding a dominating
69: vertex to G increases |V (G)|, χ(G) and ω(G) each by 1.
70:    Our main result is the following.
71: Theorem 1.4. Let k ≥ 5. If n is large enough, then Q(n, n − k) ≥ n − 2k + 3.
72: Corollary 1.5. Conjecture 1.3 is true for k ≤ 6.

1:                                                          The chromatic gap and its extremes
2: 
3: 
4: 
5: 
6: arXiv:1108.3444v1 [math.CO] 17 Aug 2011
7:                                                                               András Gyárfás∗
8:                                                                  Computer and Automation Research Institute
9:                                                                         Hungarian Academy of Sciences
10:                                                                    Budapest, P.O. Box 63, H-1518, Hungary
11:                                                                               gyarfas@sztaki.hu
12: 
13: 
14: 
15:                                                                                  András Sebő
16:                                                                          CNRS, Laboratoire G-SCOP,
17:                                                              46 avenue Félix Viallet, 38031 Grenoble Cedex, France
18:                                                                         andras.sebo@g-scop.inpg.fr

61:    The central notions in our investigations are the gap-critical and the gap-
62: extremal graphs. A graph G is gap-critical if for every proper induced subgraph
63: H ⊂ G, gap(H) < gap(G) and gap-extremal if it is gap-critical with as few
64: vertices as possible (among gap-critical graphs of the same gap). The strong
65: perfect graph theorem, solving a long standing conjecture of Berge that stimu-
66: lated a broad area of research, states that gap-critical graphs with gap 1 are the
67: holes (chordless odd cycles of length at least five) and antiholes (complements
68: of holes). The next step, the complete description of gap-critical graphs with
69: gap 2 would probably be a very difficult task. As a very first step, we prove that
70: there is a unique 2-extremal graph, 2C5 , the union of two disjoint (chordless)
71: 
72:                                          2
73: 
74:       cycles of length five.

75:          In general, for t ≥ 0, we denote by s(t) the smallest order of a graph with gap
76:       t and we call a graph is t-extremal if it has gap t and order s(t). It is tempting
77:       to conjecture that s(t) = 5t with equality for the graph tC5 . However, for t ≥ 3
78:       the graph tC5 has gap t but it is not gap-extremal (although gap-critical).
79:       We shall prove that s(3) = 13, s(4) = 17 and s(5) ∈ {20, 21}. Somewhat
80:       surprisingly, after the uncertain values s(6) ∈ {23, 24, 25}, s(7) ∈ {26, 27, 28},
81:       s(8) ∈ {29, 30, 31}, s(9) ∈ {32, 33} we can show that s(10) = 35. On the other
82:       hand we can easily show that s(t) is asymptotically equal to 2t, that is, gap(n)
83:       is asymptotic to n/2. According to our main result the gap is actually equal
84:       to ⌈n/2⌉ − α(n), unless n is in an interval [R, R + 14] where R is a Ramsey
85:       number, and if this exception occurs the gap may be larger than this value by
86:       only a small constant (at most 3).
87:          The definition of s(t) does not change if we replace (covering) gap by chro-
88:       matic gap, so it can in fact be defined with the perfectness gap as well: it is
89:       the smallest order of a graph with perfectness gap equal to t.
90:          Our study provides some new properties of Ramsey-graphs themselves: it

94:

96: 
97: 1     Introduction
98: After the proof of the strong perfect graph conjecture [7], the problems concerning
99: graph families that are close to perfectness become more interesting. Here we focus
100: our attention on a parameter that we call the chromatic gap of a graph, gap(G), equal
101: to the “duality gap” of a most natural integer linear programming formulation of the
102: graph coloring problem.
103:     Graphs in this paper are undirected, their vertex set is denoted by V (G). A
104: cycle is a connected subgraph with all degrees equal to 2. A clique is a subset of the
105: vertices inducing a complete subgraph, and a stable set does not induce any edge. The
106: notations Ci and Ki will refer to cycles, respectively cliques of order i (i = 1, 2, . . .) .
107: 
108:                                               3
109: 
110:    The size of a largest clique (resp. stable set) in a graph G is denoted, by ω(G)
111: (resp. α(G)). We also speak about k-cliques or k-stable sets meaning that their

158: denote by s(t) the smallest order of a graph with gap t. A graph is t-extremal if
159: it has gap t and order s(t); it is gap-extremal, if it is t-extremal for some t. Note
160: that the empty graph has gap 0, so s(0) = 0, and — since C5 is the unique smallest
161: non-perfect graph — s(1) = 5, and C5 is the only 1-extremal graph. It will be much
162: more difficult to prove that s(2) = 10 (Theorem 5.2). It is tempting to conjecture
163: that the pattern continues and s(t) = 5t with equality for the graph tC5 , this is how
164: we started . . . However, classical Ramsey-graphs provide better bounds. We shall
165: prove that s(3) = 13, s(4) = 17 and s(5) = 21 or 20. From a general conjecture we
166: think that the true value is 21. Somewhat surprisingly, after the uncertain values
167: s(6) ∈ {23, 24, 25}, s(7) ∈ {26, 27, 28}, s(8) ∈ {29, 30, 31}, s(9) ∈ {32, 33} we can
168: show that s(10) = 35.
169:    Gap-extremal graphs are obviously gap-critical. Holes and antiholes are gap-
170: critical but if they have more than five vertices they are not gap-extremal; if they
171: have more than eleven vertices their gap is also not maximal among graphs of the
172: same order, since the gap of two disjoint C5 is 2.
173: 
174:    A large θ(G) might be the consequence of a small ω(G). But small clique number
175: may mean not too many edges, so a large α(G) too! What happens with the gap in

245: way this might still happen is α(n) = α(n − 5) + α(5), in that case n − 1, n − 4 must
246: be both Ramsey numbers — we call them (Ramsey) twins. Probably there are no

248: in [6]) conjectured R(3, m + 1) − R(3, m) tends to infinity with m. Our main results
249: are summarized as follows.

1:                                                                         On Sums of Generating Sets in Zn2

15:                                                      Let A and B be two affinely generating sets of Zn2 . As usual, we denote their Minkowski sum
16:                                                  by A + B. How small can A + B be, given the cardinalities of A and B? We give a tight answer to
17:                                                  this question. Our bound is attained when both A and B are unions of cosets of a certain subgroup
18:                                                  of Zn2 . These cosets are arranged as Hamming balls, the smaller of which has radius 1.

421:     The range of small K has received some attention as well. In the sub-critical range K < 2, the
422: exact value of F (K) is known to be F (K) = K for 1 ≤ K < 7/4 and F (K) = 87 K for 7/4 ≤ K < 2.
423: See [6, 17, 19, 26, 41]. For K ≤ 12/5 we have F (K) ≤ (2K −1)/(3K −K 2 −1) and for 12/5 < K < 4,
424: a recursive formula is available. See [5].

448:     The exponential term 22K is as in [17, 23], but the polynomial coefficient 1/K is new. Thus
449: it re-proves Conjecture 10 for r = 2 with C = 2. This bound and Example 11 determine the
450: asymptotics of F (K) up to a factor of 2. In the next section we calculate F (K) exactly, and show
451: that the gap is unavoidable and results from the oscillations in F (K).
